#2950. Kicker Smyth elevated to Saints' playing roster

Kicker Charlie Smyth has been elevated to the New Orleans Saints' playing roster and has signed a new deal with the NFL franchise.

The former gaelic footballer, from Mayobridge in Northern Ireland, has started the last two NFL games for the Saints after being promoted from the practice squad.

The 25-year-old was handed a second opportunity to be the Saints' kicker after Dan Carlson, who started the season as first choice, was released at the start of November due to mixed form.

Smyth helped the Saints to an overtime win over the Cleveland Browns on his return to the team and then kicked a late 55-yard field goal in the 20-17 defeat by the Carolina Panthers on Sunday.

NFL rules state a player from a practice squad cannot be used more than three times in a season, and Smyth has been rewarded for his impressive return.

He made his NFL debut for New Orleans last November but was released by the franchise in August.

Smyth was retained on their practice squad due to having an exemption as he came through the NFL's international player pathway.

His first game since signing his new deal is at the Chicago Bears on Sunday at 18:00 GMT.

#2949. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations

The Philadelphia 76ers are coming off several strong performances, stringing together wins and most recently earning a comeback victory over the Orlando Magic despite missing several key players. Now, they'll return to Philly to host the Milwaukee Bucks for an early afternoon affair.

The Bucks, as things stand, sit in the basement of the Eastern Conference with a 1-15 record. Fresh off trading Giannis Antetokounmpo to Miami, they've kicked off a rebuild focused on players such as Tyler Herro, Jaime Jaquez Jr., Kel'el Ware and Ryan Rollins. They also added Brayden Burries and Nate Ament as two lottery picks in a stacked draft, and now they're looking to right the ship following the departure of their franchise star.

As their record suggests, things have not gone smoothly. Myles Turner, who remains with the team, has also endured a rough start to the season amid the turmoil. While all of the young players mentioned above have shown flashes of their potential, the Bucks are still far from being a consistent, winning basketball team.

The Sixers, on the other hand, are finding their groove and sit atop the Eastern Conference. They're coming off a strong win, with several of their key players filling up the box score. LeBron James has begun to find his rhythm, while Joel Embiid has played a crucial role in several of their recent victories. VJ Edgecombe, playing without Tyrese Maxey in their last game, was instrumental in their win over Orlando, hitting what ultimately proved to be the game-winning shot while making his presence felt on both ends of the floor.

The same can be said for the Sixers' backcourt rotation, which also features Anfernee Simons and rookie Labaron Philon. Speaking of injuries, there are a few to monitor heading into this game. Dominick Barlow is probable and could make his season debut, while Adem Bona and Dean Wade remain out. For Milwaukee, Ousmane Dieng is also out.

All of this presents a great opportunity for the Sixers to extend their winning streak against a less talented opponent. Games like this won't come often on their schedule, and they'd be wise to take advantage. Early afternoon games can always get weird quickly, but the Sixers have a golden opportunity to add another win and maintain their early-season momentum. Let's see if they can get it done.

#2946. Trump says he discussed Jensen Huang with Xi; Bessent tells CNBC Nvidia chip export policy is unchanged

President Donald Trump on Friday said he and Chinese leader Xi Jinping discussed their shared affinity for former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during their talks in China this week. Earlier in the day, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said the ousted leader was not mentioned in separate talks he had with Chinese officials.

"I did discuss that with President Xi, and he said, 'Oh, brilliant, brilliant! He's a brilliant man.' He is," Trump said at a White House press conference.

Asked earlier Friday on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" if Huang, a close ally of Trump's, had come up in "any of your discussions with the Chinese," Bessent said "no," without elaborating.

A senior Trump administration official later said that both Trump's and Bessent's remarks were correct, and that both were addressing their own conversations. The official spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss those conversations.

Trump also reiterated that he would like to see Huang — who was replaced by interim CEO Colette Kress after resigning amid allegations of an extramarital affair with a Chinese spy — be restored as Nvidia's CEO.

"I would love to see Jensen be given a second chance," Trump said. "You know, we're a country of second chances, and I'd love to be able to see him. That's a very important company to us, and he put us in a position where we are literally 10 years advanced over other companies."

Nvidia's board is not considering bringing Huang back, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC earlier this week.

The president's comments came amid growing scrutiny of advanced U.S. chips, including those made by Nvidia, ending up in China either legally or by being diverted via third countries.

Both Trump's and Bessent's remarks are potentially an indication that the Trump administration may not focus on cracking down on China's efforts to acquire advanced chips.

Bessent suggested that U.S. policy allowing the sale of Nvidia's powerful H200 AI chips to China remains unchanged following the talks in Shenzhen this week.

"We've already agreed that the ... H200s are the threshold level of chips that could be sold to the Chinese, and, you know, that continues as planned," Bessent said.

"I wouldn't anticipate any change in the policy," he added after CNBC's Sara Eisen noted that Nvidia said it had stopped selling H200s to China.

The administration ignoring the subject of Huang's affair with a possible Chinese spy — whose Singapore-based company Megaspeed has been accused of acting as a cutout for Beijing — contrasts with the importance Trump has placed on beating China in the race to artificial intelligence dominance. It could suggest that despite his support for the U.S. AI industry, Trump is less concerned with addressing China's ongoing efforts to acquire U.S. semiconductors.

Trump has also defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who has come under fire from multiple directions for reportedly slow-walking export restrictions on Chinese chip sales. "I understand both sides of it," Trump said on Monday when he also said he's like to see Huang back as CEO.

Bessent's appearance on CNBC came after Trump's return from a tour through Asia that saw him meet with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen, China.

The meeting was historic as the first time the leaders of the three countries had met without other nations' counterparts. But the trilateral summit appeared to yield few deliverables on any number of the most pressing global issues related to the countries, including the Ukraine and Iran wars or the ongoing AI race.

Bessent told CNBC on Friday that there was a "robust discussion" between Trump and Xi on trade policy and AI.

They "agreed that humans should always be involved in any military decisions that SI makes, and the SI should not be allowed to be autonomous for major military actions," he said, abbreviating the term "super intelligence," Trump's attempted rebrand of AI.

In a CNBC appearance last week, Bessent lamented the "leakage" of U.S.-made AI chips to China and blamed the Commerce Department, led by Lutnick, for slow-walking export controls on suspected Chinese cutouts like Megaspeed.

Multiple Trump administration leaders have long sought to impose export restrictions on Megaspeed, but Commerce "has the pen" and "you will have to ask them about their internal processes," Bessent said in that interview.

On Friday, however, Bessent was much more tight-lipped.

Asked if there is a dispute between him and Lutnick over how aggressively the U.S. should be pursuing Chinese export controls, Bessent said, "I think we're all committed to following President Trump's instructions."

"I think we are going to see that the ... proper chip restrictions are in place, and the remote access has been curtailed," he added. Commerce recently took steps to close one of China's paths to accessing AI processors.

Correction: Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent spoke to CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." A previous version of this story misstated the name of the show.

Editor's note: This story and headline have been updated following additional information provided by the Trump administration about the nature of Bessent's and Trump's meetings in China.

#2945. Steelers need more from DK Metcalf, other highly paid players

The Pittsburgh Steelers are 6-3 and still well positioned to make the NFL playoffs because of a favorable schedule.

For that reason, the 38-18 loss to the Cincinnati Bengals on Nov. 15 was not a total disaster and could plausibly be written off as just a bad day against a red-hot offense led by leading MVP candidate Joe Burrow.

At least that might be true if the team's most highly paid players were producing at the levels they're paid to. They decidedly are not, and until they are, then the Steelers still profile as an upper middle class AFC team capable of reaching the postseason, but probably not winning once they get there.

Required reading: Steelers' veteran CB was torched by Joe Burrow in loss to Bengals: PFF grades

Receiver DK Metcalf, who's in Year 2 of a five-year, $150 million deal, is the chief offender. Through nine games, he's on pace for the least productive season of his eight-year NFL career. It would break the lower threshold that he established last just year, his first in Pittsburgh, when he had 850 yards.

Pro Football Focus data faults him for six drops, a figure that feels generous given how frequently he fails to come down with catchable balls from quarterback Aaron Rodgers.

The contested catch numbers encapsulate the profound struggles better. PFF credits him with receptions on just 10 of 25 opportunities in 2026. That's a 40% contested catch rate, which is the worst in the NFL among receivers with at least that many opportunities. Only eight out of 38 receivers with at least 50 targets are worse overall.

Within that cohort, Metcalf ranks 33rd in yards per route run at 1.24; 32nd in yards after the catch with 125; and dead last in raw catch percentage when targeted at 50.7%.

Week 3: Denver Broncos cheerleaders perform during the "Sunday Night Football" game against the Los Angeles Rams at Empower Field at Mile High.

By no metric has he been the reliable No. 1 option that he's being paid to be. Granted, it's certainly not helped that key offseason acquisition Michael Pittman Jr. hasn't been around much to draw coverage to the opposite side of the field. Still, for $150 million, you expect a player to be able to transcend his circumstances more often than Metcalf has.

He finished the game against the Bengals with 54 yards and a touchdown but was blanked before the game got out of hand. He simply hasn't been good enough.

Linebacker/pass rusher T.J. Watt is also worthy of scrutiny. In two games against Cincinnati this season, the Steelers' $42 million man this season generated zero pressures on Burrow. Not just zero sacks. No hurries, and no quarterback hits. Needless to say, that's not good when Burrow is the man he's primarily meant to disrupt.

Four of his eight sacks this season came in the first two games. And he's on pace for the fewest tackles of his career with nine total so far, meaning he's contributing little unless he's getting to the quarterback.

There are certainly games that he's taken over. His interception return for a touchdown sealed the Week 1 win against Atlanta. His two sacks against Cleveland in Week 8 helped give his team the outright division lead momentarily.

PFF, however, has graded him at 63.8/100 or worse in five of his last seven games. The consistency is nowhere close to where it was when he was winning Defensive Player of the Year honors and setting the NFL sack record.

Beyond those two, you can find fault in a lot of the more highly paid players' respective games.

Tight end Pat Freiermuth has often been invisible as a receiving target with Pittman out because of injury. He's caught just two passes in each of the past four games, and his production against Cincinnati came mostly at garbage time.

Pittman, obviously, hasn't been available much at all because of his foot injuries. He's the offense's third-highest paid player behind Metcalf and Rodgers and has just seven catches for 89 yards in three games.

Linebacker Alex Highsmith, has just one half sack in the past seven games. His cap hit is $17 million in 2026. That problem compounds itself, too, because that lack of production will likely hurt his trade value in the offseason, when the Steelers might be looking to unload him because of big commitments to Watt and fellow outside backer Nick Herbig.

And Rodgers himself was not sharp at all against Cincinnati. He didn't complete a pass for most of the first half. He'd thrown just two touchdowns in regulation in the previous three games. And his two scoring tosses against the Bengals came once the game was well out of reach. He's in a definitive slump himself.

Add it all up, and the Steelers are relying more heavily on their depth than they probably should be in Year 1 of the transition to Mike McCarthy as coach.

If this franchise is going to win its first playoff game since 2016 come January, the guys making the most need to begin playing like it. Otherwise, another disappointment like Sunday night's could be the start of a long winter once again.

#2943. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

#2942. Warsh wants Fed officials to talk less. Instead, they’re talking more

In his first six months as Federal Reserve chairman, Kevin Warsh has diligently avoided signaling where he sees monetary policy and interest rates headed. His colleagues have been only too happy to fill the void.

Fed officials have been rushing to the microphones lately, voicing sometimes conflicting opinions on how the central bank should be handling an inflation bug that the economy can't seem to shake.

Most agree that rates should be raised — and soon. Some are willing to hike at a measured pace as conditions play out, while others, such as Governor Christopher Waller and St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem, entertain the thought of more aggressive hikes in a shorter period of time.

Largely on the sidelines in the debate is Warsh, who has expressed disdain for that sort of forward guidance and argued that it handcuffs policymakers long before they actually have to make a decision. But that hasn't stopped most of the other 18 officials who sit in on Federal Open Market Committee meetings.

In fact, according to a Bank of America analysis, there has been more so-called Fedspeak in the past two weeks than in any similar period since the start of 2025.

"Warsh's refusal to guide markets might be encouraging the rest of the FOMC to speak more, not less," Bank of America economist Aditya Bhave said in a note Thursday.

"There is always a range of views across the 19 FOMC participants. Typically, the Chair would weigh in, indicating to markets whether the hawks or doves are in ascendence," he added. "Absent this leadership, however, both sides might feel incentivized to speak more often and more forcefully."

The increasingly vocal debate comes at a critical time for the Fed and has myriad policy implications.

Bhave noted "a deepening divide" on the FOMC. The Fed's decision not to increase its benchmark rate at its October meeting "increasingly looks like a mistake," and as a result "the hawks have been very vocal of late," he wrote. Leading the charge is Waller, who has raised the possibility of hiking by half a percentage point when the committee next meets in December.

Minutes from the October meeting hint at the tension, particularly when it comes to Warsh's refusal to provide guidance.

The summary related that some officials who were looking for a hike in October "could have supported no change in the federal funds rate provided that it was clearly understood by market participants that further tightening was likely" — a statement that points toward the forward guidance that Warsh has insisted the Fed avoid.

The minutes "revealed that some officials favoring a hike would have accepted a hold if the Fed had clearly communicated further tightening," market veteran Ed Yardeni wrote Thursday. "Warsh's communication philosophy may thus be limiting the Fed's flexibility precisely when it needs it most."

Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack, who voted against the October pause in favor of a quarter-point hike, tiptoed around the forward guidance issue during a recent public appearance.

Prefacing some observations she made about where she sees policy headed, Hammack noted that, "Those are not promises. Those are not commitments. They're not forward guidance, which is a hot-button topic. But what they are are a conditional set of expectations of where I think the economy can go, based on what I'm seeing."

Hammack's comments illustrate a needle that Fed officials have been trying to thread: continuing to be transparent about their thinking without appearing locked into a future policy that changing economic conditions could render obsolete.

Speaking Thursday, Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee framed the argument around credibility.

"I am sympathetic to the view that [Warsh] has expressed that let's not be so, in in our language, forward guidance-oriented, saying here is what I think rates are going to be at the next meeting," Goolsbee said, calling such pronouncements "falsely precise."

However, he quickly separated forward guidance from transparency.

"I think central banks should be careful about doing anything that undermines credibility," Goolsbee said. "That said, I also think that it's pretty important [that] each person who sits around that table explain how they see the world. What do you see in business conditions? You don't want to surprise everybody."

Taking a slightly different tack, Philadelphia Fed President Anna Paulson, in a speech Thursday on policy communication, insisted that markets can figure out where policymakers are going without specific guidance so long as they understand how economic developments will influence decisions.

Paulson also defended the variety of voices coming from the Fed.

"But volume is not the same as clarity," she said. "The measure is not how much we say. It is whether we are understood." The mixture of voices "guards against any single communication becoming the only one that matters."

Financial markets have taken the plethora of Fed communications and interpreted it in a fairly hawkish way.

Traders are implying a 100% chance of at least a quarter percentage point hike in December, with a very small probability of a half-point move, according to the CME Group's FedWatch. From there, odds are high for a follow-up increase in January, then perhaps one more before the end of 2027.

While current inflation indicators are running above 3%, compared to the Fed's 2% target, market measures of longer-term inflation have declined.

Five-year forward expectations, as indicated through the Treasury market, are at their lowest since April. That suggests investors could be less concerned about inflation over the longer term, even as policymakers warn that current price pressures remain elevated.

Still, a sclerotic communications strategy from the Fed amid volatile economic and geopolitical currents could prove dangerous ahead.

"The result of this back-and-forth is undue rates volatility in already turbulent times," BofA's Bhave said.

For his part, Warsh has been advocating for a "family fight" atmosphere inside the Fed, arguing that competing viewpoints deserve a full airing as the committee works towards decisions.

"There are benefits to shaping the market's future expectations. Perhaps this is part of the 'family fight' that Warsh keeps referencing in his pressers," said Tom Graff, chief investment officer at advisory firm Facet. "That family fight seems to be happening out in public."

#2941. Knicks Bulletin: ‘Sometimes, it just happens’

The Knicks face the Ja-less Blazers today.

New York will host this and four more games before the Knicks pack their bags again.

Here are a few quotes for you sickos sticking to the screen even on a sunny Saturday (as if I wasn't one).

On experimenting with Jose Alvarado alongside Jalen Brunson to give opposing defenses a different look:

"Just trying to give defenses a different look. Getting Jalen off the ball some, because if you think about it, Jose played with Jalen. Tyler was the backup, he was on the floor when Jalen nor Jose was on. Jose was on the floor with Jalen, and it allowed us still to match up and move Jalen around a little bit."

On why playing Alvarado alongside Brunson has reduced Miles McBride's minutes:

"The reality of it is, if Deuce plays and we still try to do that, then Jose is not playing and Jalen is back on the ball. We'll keep trying to mix it up to see what works best for us as we go forward. It could be that or it could be Jose, because it's been two games now I think that we did it, we did it that way a little bit in Toronto, too. We felt it worked well for us."

On the Knicks finishing their road trip 3-1 despite struggling to close out the Timberwolves:

"It's a win. Three and one on the road trip. So we'll take it."

On the Knicks finding their rhythm and improving their defensive execution:

"I feel like we're getting into a good rhythm offensively, defensively. I think you'll see some of the defensive rotations were on point. Obviously, we can be better. But we're continuing to learn, gain experience. Every year is different, every team is different. So I think we're doing that, and I think we're getting better. Whenever you win in the league, you continue to build confidence."

On the Knicks proving they can win games in different ways:

"It shows that we're able to win in different ways. And that's what you have to do in the league. Some days, you're gonna shoot the cover off the ball and you can win by 10, 15, 20. Other games, it might be a possession game and offensively it's not really clicking, not knocking down shots, but you're getting stops defensively and you're able to pull out stops like that. I think it shows that this team can win games in different fashions. And that's the most important thing in the league."

On the Knicks rebuilding confidence by winning close games in different ways:

"For us to have a great road trip like this and win in so many different ways, it [says] a lot about our team. It's great when you can find a way to win. At the end of the day, it's hard to win in the NBA. I've learned that. It's hard to win. And you just have to find a way to win every single night. For us on this road trip to continue to find ways to win, it speaks to this team, and it's the most important thing when you play."

On staying ready despite an unpredictable role in the Knicks' guard rotation:

"When the time comes, you can't really explain the situation sometimes. Sometimes, it just happens. When your number is called you just always gotta be ready, so that's what the NBA has been for me. I'm just glad to be in a situation where I could at least get an opportunity or at least make my voice heard and my game played."

On why Marlon Humphrey's criticism of Baltimore felt personal to him:

"I've been sitting with this one for a couple of days, because Baltimore isn't a talking point to me. It's my blood."

On witnessing the impact of crime and systemic problems while growing up in Baltimore:

"I saw what this city can take from you. I lost friends to the streets, and I watched people I loved get swallowed by a system that was built to swallow them. I know the reputation. I've heard every joke and every headline my whole life."

On accepting Humphrey's apology but demanding more meaningful action:

"Marlon, I see the apology. I'll take it, man to man. But Baltimore has been apologized to its whole life. Apologies don't build anything. Presence does… You don't get to rep Baltimore on Sundays and talk about it like an outsider on Wednesday. Baltimore doesn't make criminals."

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released results from a survey of senior financial officers at banks about their strategies and practices for managing reserve balances. The Senior Financial Officer Survey is used by the Board to obtain information about banks' reserve balance management strategies and practices, their expectations around the size of their balance sheets and deposit balances their activity in money markets, and their views on other matters, such as operations on weekends and holidays.

The survey was conducted in collaboration with the Federal Reserve Bank of New York between September 18, 2026, and September 28, 2026, and includes responses from banks that held around three-fourths of total banking system reserve balances at the time of the survey.

#2939. Roob's Observations: Why on Earth is Johnny Mundt playing more than Eli Stowers?

Roob's Observations: Why on Earth is Johnny Mundt playing more than Eli Stowers? originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

When you've lost seven in a row, there are countless questions to answer. For this Eagles team, they include: Why is Johnny Mundt playing more than Eli Stowers? What should the Eagles do with their top-10 draft pick in April? How will they replace Jordan Davis's snaps? How lackluster has the Eagles' passing game been?

We'll do our best to answer these and a number of other pressing questions facing the hapless Eagles, who are 2-7 for only the third time since 1975.

1. Someone has to explain to me why Johnny Mundt is playing more than Eli Stowers. Mundt is a 10-year veteran who has four catches for 16 yards this year and has not been close to the blocker we all expected when the Eagles signed him. For literally zero value, Mundt has played a ridiculous 220 snaps, or 24 ½ per game. Why? He brings nothing to the table. Then there's Stowers, a promising rookie who is finally healthy after dealing with a lingering hamstring injury. The Eagles invested a 2nd-round pick on Stowers, yet he's buried on the bench. In the three games since he came back healthy, Mundt has played 37 snaps and Stowers has played 32. There is absolutely no reason on Earth a 31-year-old journeyman with no receiving ability and minimal blocking prowess should be playing more than a 23-year-old rookie 2nd-round pick. None. Even if Mundt was good, the Eagles are 2-7 and this is a time to see what the young guys can do and get them as many reps as possible. Stowers got a few targets late in the Washington game and caught three passes for 53 yards, including a 34-yarder. He had more yards on that one 34-yard catch than Mundt has all year. And do you know when Mundt's last 34-yard catch was? It was in 2020. Six years and four teams ago. You're 2-7. What are we doing here? Play the young guys. Especially ones who have shown some promise. I don't know if Stowers will ever be a legit NFL tight end. But I do know we're not going to find out if he's not playing.

2. This is amazing to me: From 1986 through 1997 – over parts of 12 seasons – the Eagles went 26-0 when they had a 100-yard rusher. Randall Cunningham ran for 110 yards in a 13-11 loss to the Lions at the Vet in November 1986 and the Eagles didn't lose again with a 100-yard rusher until September of 1997, when Ricky Watters ran for 106 yards in a 21-20 loss to the Cowboys at Texas Stadium. They also had a 10-game winning streak with a 100-yard rusher from 2002 through 2005, a 13-game winning streak with a 100-yard rusher from 2013 through 2016 and a 19-game winning streak from 2020 through 2024. In all, since 1987 they're 114-24 with a 100-yard rusher. They're 27-3 in their last 30 games with a 100-yard rusher. That's totally nuts. This year? They're 0-0. No 100-yard rushers since last year.

3. Two of the bigger decisions the Eagles will have this offseason are interlocked. What to do with Jalen Hurts and what to do with what will likely be a top-10 pick – and quite possibly a top-5 pick. Let's operate under the assumption that Hurts will be healthy by next season and able to play. And for the purpose of this conversation let's also  assume the Eagles figure out a way to sign him to an extension so he's not going into the final year of guaranteed money in 2027. Because where are you going to find a better quarterback than a guy who took the team to two Super Bowls, winning one, has won 68 percent of his career starts and reached the playoffs in every full season he's played? Might be tricky to determine his value coming off this lost season, but Howie is Howie. He'll figure it out. With those two assumptions, the question becomes what do the Eagles do with such a premium draft pick if they're not going to take a quarterback, and I think it comes down to edge rusher or offensive tackle. And you can make a pretty good case for edge, and this draft is loaded at edge, I would go offensive tackle. Unless the Eagles see Markel Bell as the long-term replacement for Lane Johnson, which I doubt will be the case, that's priority No. 1. This offensive line is just a train wreck right now and Roseman's biggest offseason assignment is going to be rebuilding it. That may start with the coach, but it's got to include personnel, and if you're picking high enough to land a Jordan Seaton from LSU or Trevor Goosby of Texas that's a heck of a place to start. There's also the Jaguars' 1st-round pick, but that will obviously be way later than the Eagles' own, probably late enough to be a best-player-available pick. The Eagles have such a tradition of elite offensive tackles, with Jon Runyan, Tra Thomas, Jason Peters, Lane Johnson and Jordan Mailata. Time to find the next guy in the lineage.

4. With Jordan Davis no longer here, one guy I want to see more of is Ty Robinson, last year's 4th-round pick. Ty had a very good second half of training camp after a slow start but has only played 61 snaps, or about 6 ½ per game. I want to see him play. I don't need to see more Byron Young. He's played 19 snaps per game and barely caused a ripple. I've seen more out of Robinson in about a third the snaps. Davis averaged 36 snaps per game, and Jalen Carter is already playing 84 percent of the snaps so he can't play more. But I'd expect Moro Ojomo – who's at 60 percent and 42 snaps per game – to add about 10 snaps a game and Young to add maybe five and then whoever is the fifth guy – practice squad call-up Leki Fotu or Gabe Hall – is going to play about 10 snaps a game. So that should leave maybe a dozen more snaps for Robinson. I like the player and want to see more of him.

5. Here's a stat that really illustrates how lifeless the Eagles' passing game has been. The Eagles are averaging 9.7 yards per completion, which would equal the lowest in franchise history over a full season. They also averaged 9.7 in 1998. The only other year they were under 10 is 1974, when they finished at 9.8. The Eagles are last in the NFL with just 14 pass plays of 20 yards or more. Only five of those in the second half. Their last 2nd-half completion of 20 yards to a WR was in Week 2, when Jalen Hurts had a 20-yarder to Dontayvion Wicks and a 23-yarder to Darius Cooper. Man, this offense is just brutal.

6. Quez Watkins caught a 26-yard pass from Daniel Jones Thursday night in the Colts' loss to the Texans, and I know people around here aren't particulary fond of Quez and I get it. His Super Bowl drop was inexcusable. His fumble after a 51-yard catch cost the Eagles a win against the Commanders. His refusal to go hard for the ball cost Gardner Minshew two interceptions in a game against the Cowboys. His last few years here were a disaster. I get all that. But here's the thing. I'm just a fan of guys who don't give up no matter how extreme the odds, and Watkins never gave up on his NFL dream despite not playing a snap in 2024 or 2025 or the first half of 2026 and getting released by four teams. This is a dude who was a 6th-round pick in 2020 and had a decent year in 2021 with 43-for-647 before his career began to spiral downhill. The Eagles even brought him back last year on the practice squad for a bit, and despite all the high-leverage mistakes I found that he had a great attitude when I chatted with him after he got here in September: "Yeah, the last couple years have just been very stressful for me," Watkins said at his locker. "But at the same time, I'm still pushing, still going and trying to get back to where I was. I definitely have a lot of football left. It's just a matter of when. When my time comes, when my time comes back around, I'll be ready to ball again." The 26-yarder Thursday night was his longest catch since a 30-yard touchdown from Jalen Hurts in a win over the Packers at the Linc in November 2022. Four years ago. Easy to dislike a guy like Quez Watkins, and I don't blame anyone who does. Me, I can't help admiring his determination and resilience. He's a survivor, and in the NFL that's not an easy thing to be.

7. Joe Milton had a pretty funny answer when asked about the nickname Bazooka Joe, which cleverly combines his first name with a metaphor for his massive arm. "I don't like it," he said. Why not? "Because bazookas only travel so far." What does he prefer? "Mr. Rocket."  You might remember that Randall Cunningham told the beat guys after the 1995 playoff loss in Dallas – his last game as an Eagle – that he nearly named his newborn son "Johnny Rocket." Randall put four names into a hat including "Randall Jr." and "Johnny Rocket," and his wife Felicity – fortunately – picked Randall Jr., born in Las Vegas the day before that 30-11 loss, went on to become a two-time NCAA Division 1 high jump champ for USC. And Randall didn't play again for two years, until Brian Billick and the Vikings revived his career.

8. Sixteen of the 27 all-time field goals of 55 yards or longer against the Eagles – 59 percent – have been made since Nick Sirianni became head coach. Kickers have made more field goals of 55 yards against the Eagles this year (five) than the first 77 years of the team's existence (four). From 1933 through 2017, opposing kickers were 6-for-23 against the Eagles from 55 yards and out (26 percent). Since 2018, they're 21-for-28 (75 percent). Ten of the 27 55-yarders against the Eagles have come since opening day last year.

9. How wild is it that the Patriots' leading receiver who's a former Eagle isn't A.J. Brown but Mack Hollins, still going strong in his 10th NFL season. Hollins, the Eagles' 4th-round pick in 2017, missed three games this year but has 23 catches for 293 yards in six games compared to Brown's 11-for-128 in four games. Hollins, who won a Super Bowl ring with the Eagles as a rookie, has really had a remarkable career with six teams, catching 231 passes for 2,912 yards and 18 touchdowns after a very slow first few years. He's 33 now and has the 4th-most catches ever by a WR after his 32nd birthday drafted by the Eagles behind Hall of Famers Harold Carmichael and Tommy McDonald plus DeSean Jackson. Good ol' Mack Hollins. Still at it.

10. When Kyle McCord started for the Dolphins against the Colts Sunday, he became only the fifth quarterback drafted by the Eagles in the sixth round or later to start an NFL regular-season game. The first was Dick Jamieson, the Eagles' 25th-round pick out of Bradley in 1959. He started only one game in the NFL, for the New York Titans in 1960 and won it, 27-3 over the Bills at the Polo Grounds. Bob Berry – the Eagles' 11th-round pick out of Oregon in 1964 – started 52 games with the Vikings and Falcons from 1966 through 1974. Koy Detmer, a 7th-round pick in 1997 out of Colorado, went 3-5 in eight career starts with the Eagles, and Tanner McKee, a 6th-round pick out of Stanford in 2024, is now 1-4 in five career starts. The Eagles have only drafted 22 quarterbacks in franchise history who have started an NFL game. Of the 49 quarterbacks who've started a game for the Eagles, only 14 were drafted by the Eagles.

#2938. Data center construction costs soar 13% in 2026

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

The demand for large-scale data centers in the U.S. this year, driven by the need for computing power for artificial intelligence, grew well beyond the supply of powered land and the labor to build these structures. It also pushed timelines and risk for contractors.

All that resulted in costs rising 13% year-over-year to more than twice the global rate, according to a new report from Turner & Townsend, a global project management company. The gain this year was about three times the last ten-year average.

The report looked at over 500 live or recently completed projects in 62 global markets. It also included a survey of 400 experts in the global data center industry.

While there was some stabilization in cost inflation this year, it was not enough to offset the huge growth in not just demand but speed of construction and delivery.

"More aggressive schedules tie to contractors in particular and subcontractors baking in more risk allowances in their pricing," said Paul Barry, North America data center lead for Turner & Townsend. "So whether that's acceleration allowances, whether that's more overtime and labor attraction allowances, even just higher fees for taking on the risk of delivering at a faster pace than what they would traditionally take on."

All of those things, combined with the spike in demand and the strain on the supply chain, translate into higher costs, he added.

Capital expenditure on global data centers is projected to rise from about $800 billion this year to $1.8 trillion per year in 2050, according to a recent report from PwC. The U.S. is expected to capture almost half of all global investment in AI infrastructure, which includes both data centers and chips.

Power is, of course, key to all data centers, and 56% of those surveyed by Turner & Townsend globally said it was a major obstacle to delivery. Just over half said utility connections typically take between one and two years, and almost a third reported timeframes of over two years. Costs for power vary regionally — from $15.01 per watt in Silicon Valley to $10.51 per watt in Dallas, based on a typical 60-megawatt design.As with every type of construction in the U.S., labor continues to be a significant headwind. Just over one quarter of respondents said their contractor capacity, that is, the ability to manage construction backlogs, was sufficient.

Going forward, Barry said he expects costs to moderate somewhat, with cost increases in the 6% to 10% range in the next year. A lot of the major projects have already started or are starting now, so cost risk has been baked into those contracts.

"If this demand continues, then there'll be more continued flow of project starts and so on, but I think we've hit a big surge in 2026, especially of new big major projects that have been procured," said Barry.

CNBC's Property Play with Diana Olick covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, delivered weekly to your inbox.

#2937. Federal Reserve announces College Fed Challenge winners

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

#2936. Hawks excel off of turnovers, eliminate Hornets in NBA Cup win

The Atlanta Hawks picked up another victory in the NBA Cup on Friday night, eliminating the Charlotte Hornets from cup progression with a 111-97 victory at State Farm Arena. Jalen Johnson led the Hawks with 26 points while Aaron Wiggins added 19 points. For the Hornets, Moussa Diabate scored 21 points, Brandon Miller added 19 points.

After suffering a disappointing loss to the Bulls in overtime in their last NBA Cup appearance, the Hawks were presented a good opportunity to steady their position in the standings with a home tie against a struggling Charlotte side. Naturally, it was the visitors who were in the ascendancy for the majority of the first half. Rallying behind their size, the Hornets secured 12 offensive rebounds leading to 20 second chance points in the first half alone, contributing to the Hornets taking not just double-digit lead in the second quarter but a lead that ran as high as 14 points.

"Diabate was part of it," said Hawks head coach Quin Snyder of the Hornets' rebounding prowess. "Not only do they have he and Kalkbrenner, two guys that it's very difficult to keep them off glass by yourself. If you are able to put a second body on, which we didn't do enough. When we did, he may not get it, but then everybody's got to clean it up. The first part of that, we need to do better. The second part of it, I'd like to get to the second part where he actually doesn't get it (the rebound). A credit to him as far as how relentless he is on the glass. I thought as the game went on, we had some possessions, and those would be guard rebounds. I think RayJ got five (rebounds), he's the smallest guy on the court. We've got to have everybody in the mix to be effective on the defensive glass."

A cold stretch from the Hornets allowed the Hawks the opportunity to chip away at the lead; by the end of which the Hawks had whittled the lead down to just two points heading into the locker room. This momentum carried through into the second half, with the Hawks not only to re-take the lead but established a double-digit lead by the end of the third quarter, progressing to a lead as high as 19 points in the fourth quarter. While a Hornets run briefly brought the lead down to 10 points, it never dipped below double-digits and the Hawks would go on to secure a comfortable victory.

Postgame, Snyder was asked about the Hornets being limited to 40 second half points, and outlined the details that the Hawks tightened up on in the second half.

"I think our rotations were not sharp early, we were just a step behind," said Snyder of the second half. "That's really important for us in our pick-and-roll defense. As we're trying to keep the ball out of the paint, we're going to find ourselves in those situations. Some of it was close-outs, when we switched pick-and-roll we weren't aggressive enough early. They shot just right behind the screen. I just think we weren't as sharp as we needed to be."

One of the keys to victory in this contest were the Hawks' points off of turnovers, scoring 36 off of the Hornets' 17 turnovers.

On the drive and pass in the air from Coby White, Dyson Daniels pounces on the loose pass, and shovels the ball ahead to Nickeil Alexander-Walker for the basket:

On this drive, it's Jalen Johnson who comes up with the knockaway, and leads the break himself in transition to dunk at the rim:

Here, Moussa Diabate operates looking for a hand-off, and his pass is telegraphed by Aaron Wiggins, who escapes Naz Reid to finish at the rim:

Again, the Hawks are active on the perimeter and Daniels procures another steal and hands it off to Alexander-Walker, who pitches it to Johnson, who finishes at the rim again:

RayJ Dennis and Zuby Ejiofor combine on this defensive play to produce another Hornets turnover, from which Dennis gets into his runner for another basket in the third quarter:

Another knock-away from Dennis sets up another Atlanta fastbreak, this time Dennis lifts the alley-oop to Ejiofor for a highlight play:

With Kingston Flemings, Jock Landale, Asa Newell, and Mo Gueye absent, there were opportunities that may not normally be available for RayJ Dennis and Zuby Ejiofor. Both players made meaningful impacts in their time on the court, and we've looked at a few already with the turnovers.

Ejiofor played 13 minutes and scored five points, but his impact goes beyond the boxscore as Snyder outlined postgame.

"I think it's timely as well," said Snyder of Ejiofor's energy off the bench. "There's plays, whether it's a block— you see him dunk but what you don't notice all the time is he sprinted to get to that position. Those are the things ,in my mind, that are hard, because he's running without the promise of a reward. Sometimes for gratification, it's hard. The things that our team needs to do to be good, that's consistent, there's not a payout in the moment. You have to run, even when you don't get the ball, you have to get a hit, even when you don't get the rebound, you have to space, even if you don't get the shot. Those things are important to him. As a result, he puts himself in position to make those kinds of plays. It's the combination of the two things together because what he's doing is selfless, and then he actually gets rewarded for it. It's a really good thing. It's something we talk about and I want our whole team to emulate.When we do it, we're a much better team."

The play Snyder was referring to, I believe, is this one, where Ejiofor runs the floor and receives the pass for the dunk in transition:

Ejiofor did not back down when confronted with Diabate while on the floor; it was this, in addition to the energy he provided, that stood out to the Hawks last night.

"It was good, rook coming in, you know, not backing down," said Dyson Daniels of Ejiofor. "It's what we like, another big fella getting the mix with Mo (Gueye) being out, that's usually Mo doing that sort of thing. So, Zuby in there, it was really cool to see. He's just come in, giving us really good minutes, giving us good energy around the floor, and he's making some big shots for us. He's really stepped up since Jock's gone down, which has been good."

Dennis, similarly, played 13 minutes, scoring eight points but, similar to Ejiofor, his impact was felt beyond his boxscore.

"It's great when you have guys in the program," said Snyder of RayJ Dennis. "Last year in College Park, this summer, throughout the course of September, he's got a good feel for how we want to play. He played well in Oklahoma City, is all he didn't make a shot. He can get in the lane and when he gets in there he's capable of finishing. He also has good vision, I think he's worked on that, too. Big minutes from him tonight gave us a lift."

Without CJ McCollum and and Kingston Flemings in the lineup, the Hawks have been operating without a primary point guard. It was Dyson Daniels who stepped up with 10 assists last night to lead the Hawks from a playmaking perspective. Daniels himself believe it has been done by committee.

"I think everyone's stepped up," said Daniels of the point guard play without McCollum and Flemings. "Obviously, we don't have like Kingston or CJ, like a true point guard out there but we've just been pushing the pace. Everyone's getting touches, we've got guys that can play with the ball, guys that can play off the ball. So I think everyone's just filled those voids. Guys like RayJ (Dennis) have been really good coming in as well, playing, some good minutes for us. Corey (Kispert) coming as well, playing some really good minutes. Everyone's kind of stepped up, which has been good."

While Daniels starred in his own role, there was little doubt as to who the MVP was for the Hawks last night, and that was Jalen Johnson. Johnson scored 26 points on 10-of-15 shooting from the field and 3-of-4 from three:

As we looked at earlier, Johnson excelled in transition/off of Charlotte turnovers. The manner of which he was able to manoeuvre through bodies in transition to get to the rim looked effortless at times; he just looked in total command in the open court.

It hasn't been an easy season for Johnson shooting the three-pointer, shooting 22% on the season, and while it was encouraging to see Johnson hit 3-of-4 from, the poor percentages so far are't going to dissuade the Hawks and Johnson from shooting.

"I think he knows that we want him to keep shooting," said Snyder of Johnson. "The catch-and-shoots that he hit were quick decisions. I thought the thing that he did tonight where he was really impactful was he played without the ball. That's important for him and for us, particularly when people are game-planning for him, whether it was a matchup on the ball, his ability to do both, to get out in transition, to get to the foul line, to offensive rebound, all those things add up. I think it calms your nervous system a little bit, too. He knows how we feel about him. His shooting the ball is important for us, so it's good to see a couple go in, no question about it. I don't want to obsess about it either because there's lots of ways he can help us."

"I mean it happens," added Johnson of his three-point shooting. "Obviously I haven't shot the ball the way I wanted to but I put in the work. I'm not worried about the percentage or whatnot, I know my shot is going to fall. It was good to see a couple go in for sure. I'm going to keep shooting the shots that I work on and just continue to let them fall."

The Hawks move to 2-1 in the NBA Cup and are currently 2nd in the group, trailing the Boston Celtics who have played just two cup games (2-0). The Hawks and Celtics will face-off next Friday at the TD Garden, the winner practically guaranteed to advance from Group C. The Hawks are obviously motivated to win, but the mechanisms of the NBA Cup provide another incentive to win.

"I mean, obviously we want that money," said Jalen Johnson when asked how the Hawks prepare for a cup game. "So, I think it's as simple as that. Obviously we understand that the stakes are higher considering what's for the reward. So, I think we all young, we all like an extra prize and money. So, I think just upping our intensity, upping our focus."

The Hawks (10-6) are back in action on Sunday afternoon where they host the Detroit Pistons (10-5) at State Farm Arena.

#2935. Trump vows to stop U.S. companies from building data centers abroad, threatens 'tremendous tax'

President Donald Trump on Friday said U.S. companies will be banned from building data centers in other countries and will face a "tremendous tax" penalty if they do so.

"We're going to fight very hard for the data centers," Trump said at a White House press conference before announcing the new policy — which apparently came as news to his own staff.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story," Trump said. "I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now they're here."

Trump's latest push for more data-center construction in the U.S. came despite Americans repeatedly stating — and showing with their votes — that they dislike the structures.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey found that 64% of registered voters opposed an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% of respondents said they would support one.

If Trump follows through on his threat to clamp down on overseas data center construction, he will have to contend with the fact that numerous U.S. tech giants — including Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Meta and Oracle — have already built them in other countries.

International expansion represents a growth opportunity for the top cloud infrastructure providers, which want to serve clients from data centers that are nearby, sometimes to meet regulatory requirements.

Trump said Friday, however, that if companies want to continue building the centers outside the U.S., "they can, but we're going to put a tremendous tax on them."

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy, and they have everything. They're tremendous assets," Trump said.

Representatives at cloud providers Amazon, Google, Microsoft and Oracle did not immediately respond to requests for comment.

Trump's remarks came a day after Trump met with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin in China for a trilateral summit that appeared to produce few concrete deliverables on an array of pressing global issues.

Xi "congratulated me on how well we're doing" in artificial intelligence, Trump said, using his preferred term "super intelligence," which he said the Chinese president also likes better.

— CNBC's Jordan Novet and Annie Palmer contributed reporting.

#2929. AI is changing software engineering — and some developers miss the struggle

When Gemma Rodgers began her computer science degree in 2002, software engineering was seen as a future-proof and lucrative career. It appealed to people who enjoyed getting into the weeds of code, tracking down bugs and building programs from scratch.

It's an industry that has always moved fast, Rodgers, a software developer based in the U.K., told CNBC. But the arrival of artificial intelligence has accelerated the pace, with AI tools now part of the daily work of every software engineer she knows. In a 2026 Stack Overflow survey, 83% of software developer respondents said they use at least one AI tool.

As AI coding tools help engineers write, debug and test code faster than ever, some are mourning the job they used to have. Rodgers has always loved the sense of accomplishment that comes from piecing together different parts of code to find a solution, particularly when she's spent days, or even weeks, trying to fix a stubborn bug.

"The sense of joy and fun that I used to experience by having a challenge and then having to work that out, it's been removed somewhat," she said. "Now you can sometimes do it in a few prompts, and it's great — but it's a breadcrumb of joy, not a full meal of joy."

Rodgers is not alone in feeling nostalgic about the not-so-old days. Software engineers have taken to social media to describe missing their old way of working, when they could get into a flow state with code for hours at a time.

Still, AI tools can be useful and have lowered the barrier to entry significantly. "It's becoming an industry where a lot of people have been able to thrive who wouldn't necessarily have been able to thrive 10 years ago, five years ago, even three years ago," Rodgers said.

It's also important that the core skills engineers have traditionally learned on the job are not lost, she said. AI tools and internet services can go down unexpectedly, and engineers still need to know how to investigate problems and work through solutions themselves.

"The sorts of skills that we were taught years ago are kind of being lost because [engineers are] having to just churn work out," Rodgers added.

The job itself is changing, too. James Broadhead, chief technology officer of AI decision-making company Taktile, told CNBC that engineers who might previously have spent three weeks building a prototype can now generate 10 in an afternoon. The hardest part of that, he said, is "having the judgment to decide which of those are any good."

"The [AI] agents are spitting out so much code that [engineers] haven't read it all anymore," Broadhead said. "It used to be you'd build up a sense of comfort and awareness of how the whole code base was structured, but now the whole thing changes all the time."

Joshua Wöhle, CEO of London-based AI training company Mindstone, told CNBC he sees a growing divide between workers who know how to use AI effectively and those who don't.

The industry used to talk about "10X engineers," he said, who were dramatically more productive than their peers. Now, AI is widening that gap even further to produce "100X engineers."

Wöhle believes companies will increasingly have to decide whether to help employees catch up with AI or prioritize those who can get the most out of it. But he also understands why some engineers might struggle to adapt.

The people who once excelled at the job were often those who loved writing and working through code, he said, and those are not necessarily the current top performers.

"That's the grief that is very, very real," Wöhle said. "The thing you love doing is no longer the thing the company should be paying for, to a degree, because it's no longer adding the value to the company that it used to."

But not every engineer sees the move away from hands-on coding as a bad thing. Chris Sees, a software engineer of more than 20 years and CEO of London-based virtual office company Hoxton Mix, told CNBC that AI gives developers more time to tackle interesting problems. He views AI as a capable junior engineer that still needs supervision.

"The important thing is knowing what to delegate and when human judgement is needed," Sees said. "We'd be silly to think AI gets everything right, and I know I get a lot of satisfaction out of proving and spotting where it has gone wrong."

Even so, Sees worries about what happens to the next generation of engineers if AI takes over the routine coding and debugging through which they traditionally learned. Those tasks are "where you learn lessons and build scar tissue," he said, and companies need to make sure AI becomes part of the learning process, rather than a shortcut.

For Rodgers, what matters is having control over how she uses the technology. As a self-employed developer, she can use AI to automate the parts of a project she finds tedious, while keeping the more interesting problem-solving for herself.

She believes there's something important about working through a difficult problem rather than immediately outsourcing it to technology. Friction and discomfort, she said, are vital parts of the human experience.

"As humans, we need to feel accomplished in something and part of accomplishment requires challenge," she said. "Unless we are able to work that through in so many different aspects of life, then you never get the full force of the good feeling on the other side."

#2926. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.
