#2922. Europe's cyber weak spot is the everyday businesses nobody thought to protect

Cyberattacks on refrigerators in French supermarkets in October spurred Swedish chain Coop to act. But it wasn't fast enough.

Attackers hit refrigeration systems in seven of its stores in early November. The affected stores had been scheduled for security upgrades just two days later, a company spokesperson told CNBC.

Mundane but essential equipment, used by everyday businesses, has become a favorite target of cyber saboteurs in recent weeks.

Experts are warning that a combination of known vulnerabilities, basic security failures and ambiguity over whether suppliers or their clients are responsible are making things worse, as AI makes cyber threats faster and more sophisticated.

Guy Segal, CEO of cybersecurity firm Sygnia, told CNBC that his company handled 285% more incidents in October than a year earlier, with demand forcing it to turn away some businesses seeking emergency assistance.

"It's going to get worse before it's going to get better," Segal said.

The recent cyberattacks targeted refrigeration systems across hundreds of European supermarkets, remotely altering temperature settings and forcing retailers to discard spoiled food.

France has since accused a cyber unit with links to Iran's Revolutionary Guard Corps of the attacks, an allegation Tehran denies.

Fredrik Blix, a cybersecurity researcher at Stockholm University, told CNBC that thousands of industrial control systems remain accessible over the public internet, sometimes despite manufacturers' warnings.

Segal said that basic cyber protection measures such as multi-factor authentication and basic network segmentation are more important than ever.

He said risks to major civic infrastructure like the electrical grid were front of mind and so "usually pretty well protected."

"There are always those civil supply chain components that are way, way less protected. Could be supermarkets, could be gas stations, could be… the screens on the Tube," Segal said, referring to London's underground rail system.

The supermarket attacks showed "this is the new normal, and definitely it's a wake-up call for every company out there," Segal added.

Segal said that inexpensive AI tools mean technical expertise and resources are no longer needed to launch attacks. He described one recent incident in which clients suffered breaches within six hours of a vulnerability becoming known, as perpetrators used AI to act faster — a process that might have taken weeks a year earlier.

Segal, whose firm helps companies respond to breaches and strengthen their defenses, warned that businesses without prearranged incident-response support could struggle to secure emergency assistance, as overwhelmed cybersecurity firms prioritize existing clients.

In one case, he said, Sygnia had to turn down a company because no response teams were available. Segal added that the company in question had already been turned down by ten other providers.

Jacob Silverman, CEO of risk advisory firm Kroll, told CNBC earlier this month that the company was operating at full capacity in its incident-response business amid what he described as "the most rapid uptick in threat activity that we've ever seen."

Segal argued that retailers should treat cybersecurity risks with the same seriousness as fire safety and food hygiene, including when outsourcing maintenance.

When Ahold Delhaize stores in Belgium and Luxembourg were attacked on Nov. 2, it exposed how different independent store operators had responded to the warnings following the earlier attacks in France.

Retailers had been warned about the vulnerabilities following earlier attacks in France, and those that implemented the recommended protections were spared, CEO Frans Muller told investors in an earnings call earlier this month, but smaller operators with fewer technical resources had struggled to make the necessary changes.

Coop, whose stores are owned by regional consumer cooperatives or operated by franchises, told CNBC that public attention on the recent attacks was the main driver of security improvements. It said the vulnerability at the affected stores had since been addressed.

The financial consequences of cyberattacks can be substantial. Ahold Delhaize told investors in November that a separate October cyberattack on its online operations and supply chain would reduce fourth-quarter sales by 200 million euros ($219 million).

Danish shipping giant Maersk, which suffered an estimated $250 million to $300 million in losses from the 2017 NotPetya cyberattack, has spent nearly a decade strengthening its defenses.

CEO Vincent Clerc told CNBC earlier this month that the experience had prompted significant investment in security capabilities, but warned that AI was enabling attackers to become more effective.

"Everybody is in a race" to boost defenses, he said.

Research by cybersecurity firm Censys found that the number of internet-exposed refrigeration controllers made by Danfoss and Copeland exposed to cyber hacking fell 53% globally between Oct. 9 and Nov. 5.

However, more than 3,100 remained exposed, and Censys said it could not determine whether the decline reflected permanent security improvements or equipment simply being taken offline.

A Copeland spokesperson confirmed that some of its refrigeration systems were affected by the vulnerability and said it had contacted customers to reinforce cybersecurity guidance, including keeping devices off the public internet and updating software.

Danfoss didn't respond to CNBC requests for comment.

In the U.K., a covert cybersecurity operation has been underway to protect tens of thousands of refrigeration systems at supermarkets, warehouses, restaurants and other businesses, the BBC reported on Friday. After researchers discovered a major vulnerability in late October, cybersecurity specialists, IT teams and the U.K.'s National Cyber Security Centre worked to alert dozens of companies and secure their systems before hackers could exploit the flaw.

The EU's Cyber Resilience Act will also impose stricter security requirements on manufacturers of connected products from December 2027.

But the legislation does not require all existing equipment to be made compliant with the new standards.

Blix told CNBC on Nov. 6 that European authorities should take a more proactive approach to reducing vulnerabilities by identifying exposed industrial control systems and contacting their operators before attacks occur.

"What worries me is that we don't really do anything," he said, adding that "we are shuffling a lot of papers" and "doing a lot of risk assessments" but failing to take meaningful action.

#2918. Nick Sirianni sticking with Sean Mannion, Joe Milton out of bye

Nick Sirianni sticking with Sean Mannion, Joe Milton out of bye  originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Eagles are sticking with offensive coordinator Sean Mannion and quarterback Joe Milton coming out of the bye week, head coach Nick Sirianni said on Wednesday afternoon.

Before their Week 10 bye week, the Eagles lost seven straight games with an offense that now ranks dead last in yards and 30th in scoring.

But Sirianni is sticking with Mannion as the Eagles 'play-caller going into Week 11's game against the Steelers.

"I think we had a really good bye week process," Sirianni said. "Got to evaluate a lot of things within that. I think I put into pretty good detail of kind of how we go through things and the things that present themselves as you go through it. I think we have a good plan of action moving forward of the things that we want to do to move forward and to get better. Obviously, we haven't been good enough on offense but I like our plan of attack and our process that we went through last week."

There was a lot of excitement when the Eagles hired Mannion this offseason but the offense has struggled all season. Mannion is a first-year OC, so maybe the Eagles expected some growing pains. But this has been extreme. They literally have the worst offense in the NFL. Even when you acknowledge the Eagles lost Jalen Hurts and have been really injured, that's still not acceptable.

Sirianni has previously praised Mannion for his work ethic. But aside from that, what gives the head coach confidence in Mannion?

"I think he's really smart. I think he has a great football mind," Sirianni said. "He thinks through everything. I think one thing I always value about him is anybody can come and give a play and say, 'Hey, I want to do this or I want to do that.' But I think he has a really good monotonous approach to figuring out everything that could potentially happen in that situation and what are the downfalls and what [is] the upside and do we move forward with some of those things? Again, I think that just speaks to the detail of what he has.

"Again, it hasn't went well enough for any of us this year offensively. That's coaches, that's players. We all have things that we need to do better. But I do love that approach that he has with that. As we looked at some of our schemes that he's brought in, some of those have been really, really good. Again, it has to be better as a whole. Without getting too much into the specifics of what those are. We just need to build on that."

The Eagles did recently bring back T.J. Paganetti as a consultant, but Sirianni said he didn't make any other staff changes during the bye week.

The Eagles gave Milton his first-career start before the bye week in the 19-11 loss to the Giants. While Milton did some good things, the offense still struggled, which was probably to be expected after he had been with the team for just three weeks.

Perhaps Milton will be in a better spot against the Steelers on Sunday.

"He works extremely hard with everything," Sirianni said of Milton. "Really love his approach, like his personality. But I really appreciate his approach of how he goes through everything. Coaches have done a nice job, Parks (Frazier) has done a nice job. But his approach, he's a really hard worker and I really value that."

The good news for Mannion, Milton and the offense is that Sirianni said the team is "hopeful" that DeVonta Smith (hamstring) and Dontayvion Wicks (ankle) will be able to play on Sunday. The Eagles' top two receivers missed the three games before the bye week.

#2917. A massive bearish trade just happened in the chip stocks

So much for buying the dip.As the S&P 500 entered correction territory and the Nasdaq-100 flirted with bear-market territory, down 19% from highs as of midday Friday, the single biggest options trade of the day was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) has a long way more to fall.Around 12:30 E.T., someone spent $93 million buying 20,000 380-strike puts expiring Sep. 17, 2027, according to trading data reviewed by SpotGamma and Volland, options-analytics platforms that both show the trade as a likely opening position, with volume exceeding existing open interest in the contract. The trade bets that the index will fall below $380, a nearly 8% decline from its current level. The SMH is off 40% from its recent all-time highs.

"That trade stands out big-time," wrote Jason DeLorenzo, founder and owner of Volland. "It is either a hedge or speculative, but either way it isn't a vote of confidence."The puts were bought for $46.50 a piece, an arguably expensive choice for a downside bet with volatility in the ETF trading at 70.

"You're paying a metric ton of Vega for that thing," added SpotGamma's Brent Kochuba, referring to the contract's sensitivity to changes in volatility of the underlying chip ETF. "Maybe they were a forced buyer, or maybe they're actually selling this, which can still be the case even though the price shows it happening closer to the ask."

The semiconductor index headed for a 9% drop this week alone.

#2915. The Lakers may soon experiment with a very interesting lineup

The benefit of having two elite ballhandlers is that one of them should always be on the floor. For the Lakers, when both Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves have been healthy this season, they have been staggered so that one of them is always playing.

But head coach JJ Redick and his staff are open to the idea of mixing things up where that's not the case in the future.

Ahead of the team's recent game against the Jazz, Redick was asked about Grimes' role this season and whether the team might keep him in the starting lineup even when Reaves returns from injury to help him keep his rhythm. While Redick was noncommittal on a starting lineup change, he did offer insight into another way the Lakers are looking to help the non-Luka and Reaves players find a rhythm.

"We're kind of talking about as a staff," Redick said, "but we just haven't had a stretch of time, a stretch of games to do this, is trying to find more minutes with, when we're healthy, with no Luka, no AR and and see if that can sort of get those guys going on their own. So then, second half when we kind of make those decisions in the fourth quarter about who we want to finish the game with, those guys are in a rhythm.

"I think that's probably what we're focused on once we're fully healthy rather than long term starting lineups because, again, I think with this group and the way this group, it's just an egoless group. We're going to be able to be flexible with the starting lineup and flexible with matchups."

There's not a great deal of harm in experimenting, even if it's an idea that feels a little far-fetched. The Lakers have struggled in the non-Luka minutes with Austin out, so I'm not sure it makes sense to go to those minutes when they are healthy.

The idea might make sense on paper. It gives other players more freedom. But it comes at the risk of not having elite players on the court.

We'll see whether Redick and his staff ever implement this idea. It feels like a pretty risky proposition. Is it worth potentially losing a couple games to try out this theory, especially if it doesn't work?

#2913. Why Anthropic's blockbuster IPO couldn't escape the stock market correction

No one can say the Anthropic initial public offering wasn't a success for the company. Deal size and pricing were signs of what IPO experts say was insatiable demand for "ANT" shares. But what came within a day is something that investors better get used to: anxiety in the stock market about valuations being assigned to artificial intelligence revenue and business models that should keep Anthropic shares volatile in the period ahead.

The Anthropic IPO reinforces those fears rather than running counter to them, says Avery Marquez, director of investment strategies at Renaissance Capital, which researches IPOs and manages the Renaissance IPO ETF (IPO).

"The volatility in the AI trade we've been seeing for the past few months stems from valuations and whether we are in a bubble, and that's been heightened and highlighted by the Anthropic IPO," Marquez said on "ETF Edge" on Friday.

And in the case of Anthropic, which finished trading Friday down 9%, the valuation anxiety becomes "so much more prevalent," she added, because it is a new stock.

It is not just any new stock, but one that has Wall Street adding downside risks to research notes likely never seen before, and which may be the greatest understatement (or most subtle equities research joke) in the history of earnings analysis. "Anthropic could develop AI systems which become fully autonomous and wipe out humanity which may lead to a serious decline in earnings," Seaport Research Partners analyst wrote Jay Goldberg wrote on Nov. 18.

"It was challenging company to value ... and it's very richly valued," Marquez said. "We're seeing something more reasonable and investors grappling now with where should this company actually be valued," she added.

The fact that Anthropic pulled off the deal in this market and was able to command the price it did was an achievement, according to Yuri Khodjamirian, chief investment officer at Tema ETFs, which offers multiple funds that invest in pre-IPO shares of companies and held Anthropic in its Tema Photonics & Optical ETF (LAZR) well before IPO day. "We have to remember they pulled off a very large IPO in the middle of a very severe market with the volatility in the tech sector specifically," Khodjamirian said on "ETF Edge."

Khodjamirian says the investor demand for AI remains, but amid a reassessment of returns. And he says an equally hard task has now begun for Anthropic. Investors are unwinding positions in everything from tech stocks to gold and bitcoin, and repositioning for 2027 based on their new outlooks. "We are in a bit of vacuum," he said.

"From here on the market is trying trying to figure out what direction to go in," Khodjamirian said, whether that is based on Fed policy, geopolitics, the midterm elections results, or the AI trade. "In many cases, the chief investment officers I speak to, and the clients, say 'I'm just gonna take some risk off and wait and see until I get more info.' And with the Anthropic, the revenue picture and how the AI adoption curve progresses will be a vital stat to watch," he added.

Plenty of value has already been wiped out in tech, according to Khodjamirian, but investors need new signals to buy. That doesn't just go for Anthropic but for much of the market's tech leadership. "One of the things most difficult for investors to understand right now is some of the leadership in the market that has been taken by semiconductor companies," he said, adding that the peak was 22% of S&P 500 earnings. But these companies, like Nvidia and Micron, have a very different earnings profile from the rest of the market.

The VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH), which was down over 10% on Friday and around 15% for the week, was on pace for its worst week since September 2001, according to CNBC data on Friday afternoon. It also lost 15% in one week of March 2020. The tech sector of the S&P 500, tracked by the State Street Technology Select Sector SPDR ETF (XLK), turned in its worst weekly performance since the Covid crash.

Khodjamirian said while these stocks may look cheap on a P/E basis right now after the heavy selling, that requires faith in the earnings estimates, which he says will be more important as results are released for the next few years. "Right now it looks cheap, but will companies like Micron and Nvidia actually deliver?"

Nvidia shares fell 14% on Friday, adding to its worst stretch of weekly performance since 2008. What's more, the single biggest trade in the options market on Friday was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF has a long way more to fall.

And the same caution about the outlook goes for Anthropic, he said. "That's why we need to watch Anthropic's annual recurring revenue number, because it is a key part of puzzle," he said.

Marquez said there is always hype in names like SpaceX and Anthropic when they go public, and it is hard to know whether the pricing of the deal is based on fundamentals, "but we just need more time and lots price discovery," she said. "It is not going to happen overnight," she added.

As an IPO researcher, she says this "discernment" is a healthy thing for the markets. There are few cases to look to as comps for Anthropic, other than SpaceX, and before that, way back to Facebook's IPO in 2012. And neither example is one of quick recovery. SpaceX shares remain well below their first-day closing price more than five months after its IPO. Facebook (now Meta), meanwhile, "did not do well for the first year after going public and we see where it is now. It's just the nature of these disruptive tech companies that come to market," Marquez said. "They lend themselves to more volatility and a longer period of price discovery for investors."

All eyes will next be on Anthropic's rival OpenAI, which has suggested it will be ready to go public in 2027. Marquez said the reaction to the Anthropic deal may be a signal that it was "a good idea to wait, allowing themselves to build more quarters of better financials and improve fundamentals a bit before taking on a market that is really quite discerning."

Tema does not own shares of OpenAI in any of its ETFs. But, "I can't say it wont be," Khodjamirian said. "They exist out there in the secondary market, and we will look at it, but we haven't made any decision. It depends on the ETF fit and if the portfolio manager wants to invest. We are always looking out for these interesting businesses but never just for the sake of private exposure," he added.

Tema does own Databricks, Stripe, Kalshi and Polymarket across its funds. It also was an investor in SpaceX pre-IPO through its Tema Space Innovators ETF (NASA).

Khodjamirian said demand from public market investors to access these "generational businesses" will continue to be the case, despite the market volatility, and will more often come before IPO day. In fact, he pointed to the filing from the world's largest asset manager and one of the biggest ETF companies in the world, BlackRock, earlier this week. It wants to bring out a new kind of exchange-traded product that would bring the ETF and interval fund structures together as a way to increase exposure to private companies, with shares trading on an exchange while the fund makes monthly repurchase offers.

#2912. Eagles vs. Steelers predictions for Week 11 of the 2026 NFL season

Eagles vs. Steelers predictions for Week 11 of the 2026 NFL season originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Eagles (2-7) are back from their bye week and will host the Pittsburgh Steelers (6-3) on Sunday afternoon at the Linc.

Someday this year the Eagles will win another game. I don't think it will be this one. The Steelers do a lot of things well. They're third in the NFL in run defense and seventh in scoring defense, allowing only 20 points per game, and T.J. Watt is still terrorizing quarterbacks at 32 years old. Undrafted Jaylen Warren leads the NFL at 5.7 yards per carry and is a dangerous receiver out of the backfield, future 1st-ballot Hall of Famer Aaron Rodgers can still make plays when he's protected – he's got 15 TD passes and six INTs this year – and D.J. Metcalf isn't what he used to be with Seattle but still capable of coming up big a few times a game. The Steelres are 6-3 and 4-1 in their last five and they don't have a ton of quality wins, but they aren't playing a quality team Sunday afternoon at the Linc. The Steelers aren't a Super Bowl threat, but they do a lot of things well. The Eagles don't do anything well. Except lose. They're good at that. Getting DeVonta Smith back and a healthier Saquon Barkley should help Joe Milton, but I just don't see the Eagles competing for 60 minutes with a solid team like the Steelers.

I don't think this Steelers team is as good as their record but I do think they're better than the Eagles. Even though the bye week should definitely help the Eagles find some better answers on offense as Joe Milton learns the scheme, that Steelers defense is good up front. There are some serious concerns about pass pro holding up in this game and the Steelers are hard to run against. The one advantage the Eagles have is at receiver as DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks return. But Milton will need time to throw. The Eagles' defense should be able to slow down this version of Aaron Rodgers but I just don't have a lot of faith that the Eagles will be able to snap their losing streak.

Last week's bye week was a much-needed break for this Eagles team. Players needed to heal up physically and mentally. As a player, the bye week allowed me to decompress and refocus on the season. Even when the season is going well, getting away helps the mind and body reset for the remainder of the season.

Coaches don't usually get that break. They try to figure out how to get the players performing better, fix what's going wrong and reinforce what they should continue doing right.

On offense, getting DeVonta Smith back will surely boost the passing attack for Joe Milton. Helping the O-line with protection should be the offense's No. 1 focus. Markel Bell will need help against one of the best pass rushers in the league in T.J. Watt. They must send a chipping RB, TE presence and slide the line toward him. Sean Mannion cannot let the right side of the line wreck the entire offense.

Defensively, Vic Fangio will have to call an aggressive style of play. The DBs will have to play tighter coverage or Aaron Rodgers will pick the defense apart. Stopping the run without Jordan Davis will also be a priority. A collective effort will be required to compensate for losing Davis on 1st and 2nd down. Can the Eagles keep the win streak going against the Steelers who have not won in Philadelphia since 1965? Maybe. Will they? Probably not.

Fresh off the bye week with some reinforcements on the way at receiver, you'd have to hope the Eagles offense shows some level of improvement. Against the Giants, they managed just one score and 30 of the 60 yards gained on that drive came via penalty. It has been an abysmal effort of late. Having DeVonta Smith and more time on task for new starting quarterback Joe Milton should — in theory — lead to better results.

Aaron Rodgers turns 43 next month and just lost his top interior protector on the offensive line in Mason McCormick. That should spell a big game for Jalen Carter. He tends to feast on inferior opponents. However, the Eagles are down both of their defensive captains. Zack Baun is dealing with a back injury and Jordan Davis was just dealt to the Jaguars. I suspect they'll struggle to stop the Steelers' rushing attack, led by Jaylen Warren.

The Steelers haven't won a game in Philadelphia since 1965. That changes on Sunday.

The Steelers are much better on paper than the way they are actually playing. Pittsburgh has a great defensive line… that doesn't get a lot of pressure. Talented receivers… to go along with one of the worst passing offenses in the NFL. The stats don't match up to the talent you see on the Steelers team. However, they are good at two things — running the football and winning close games. Two things the Eagles cannot seem to figure out so far this season. I am encouraged to see if the Eagles offense can make any strides with Joe Milton's second game at quarterback and the return of DeVonta Smith and Wicks. The Eagles' defensive line has a winnable matchup against a banged up Steelers offensive line but Aaron Rodgers is known for getting the ball out quick. I don't trust anything the Eagles are doing right now to be able to win this game.

It's good to have football back in the city of Philadelphia. I know it's been a frustrating campaign to say the least, but when football is in town, the city has a different type of energy to it. Against the Steelers, you know exactly the type of ballgame it's going to be. They want to play a slow, monotonous brand of football where they run the ball, churn clock, smother you defensively, and leave town with a win without doing anything spectacular. They're a veteran-laden unit headlined by future Hall of Famers in Aaron Rodgers and T.J. Watt, but it's their blend of youth-infused talent that showcases a well structured football team.

For the Eagles, I'm excited to see Joe Milton once again with what looks to be a WR core at full strength at time of writing. With extra time to prepare via the bye, I expect Milton to make some throws this week allowing his guys to make a few plays downfield. Pittsburgh is underwhelming in the secondary, and if Milton is granted just a few ticks of time to work through his progressions, the Eagles could create some explosive plays this week. Everything, however, starts with the offensive line, and I hope this coaching staff gives Markel Bell help against Watt or the longtime Steeler could have a handful of sacks by himself this weekend. That simply cannot happen. Double teams, chips, sliding protection, do something to help out your rookie RT whose confidence looks to have wavered mightily since the Jacksonville and Carolina games.

Defensively, limiting RB Jaylen Warren is the key to this week. He's the most underrated player in football in my book and the explosive engine that gets this whole thing going on offense for Pittsburgh. Limit him, and you have more than just a shot at winning this ballgame. My eyes are also on the edge rushers to create pressure on the soon-to-be 43 year old Rodgers, and the LB core which could be without Zack Baun. Shedding blocks with consistency and tackling in open space has lacked within this defense — that must improve on Sunday. Overall, I haven't seen this team adjust in months and this Steelers team is a rugged ballclub that just got embarrassed on Sunday Night football against the Bengals. Do I hope HC Nick Sirianni and OC Sean Mannion made adjustments during the bye week? Absolutely, but, my confidence in executing said changes is a whole other conversation.

#2909. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2906. An early breakdown in the polar vortex is threatening already depleted gas stocks

An unprecedented early breakdown of the stratospheric polar vortex and a record-breaking El Niño event has put energy traders on alert this winter, with analysts warning the weather and climate phenomena could have an outsized impact on Europe.

Meteorologists are currently observing a record early breakdown of the polar vortex, a large-scale circulation of strong winds that typically keeps extremely cold air over the North Pole.

Changes in the strength and shape of the polar vortex can influence weather patterns much lower down in the atmosphere, potentially bringing in colder weather to parts of Europe and North America.

This year, however, the collapse of the stratospheric polar vortex, or SPV, is taking place at the same time as an unusually powerful El Niño, a climate event that can boost global temperatures and disrupt rainfall.

The collision of these two events raises the risk of a prolonged cold spell that could lift heating demand and force European countries to draw down already-low reserves faster. That would leave the continent more reliant on imported liquefied natural gas (LNG) at a time when supplies from the Middle East are disrupted.

To be sure, Europe's natural gas storage levels started the winter at their lowest level in more than a decade.

The International Energy Agency warned earlier this month that low European storage levels and limited storage options in several LNG-importing countries in Asia and South America could intensify the competition for cargoes and put upward pressure on prices during periods of high demand.

Simon Lee, a lecturer in atmospheric science at the U.K.'s University of St Andrews, said the combination of a sudden stratospheric warming (SSW) with an El Niño event is not necessarily unusual, but meteorologists had been expecting a polar vortex breakdown in February — not as early as November.

The early SPV disruption is an "extremely rare" event that could influence winter weather in northwest Europe and the eastern U.S., Lee said, increasing the chance of colder conditions although likely not to the extent that a polar vortex event caused parts of Niagara Falls to partially freeze in January 2014.

"Geopolitics and weather are the two key volatility drives for energy and now they seem to be happening at the same time — and that's always the biggest risk for markets," Florence Schmit, senior energy strategist at Rabobank, told CNBC.

For months, meteorologists expected an El Niño event would result in a milder winter in Europe and relatively muted heating demand, Schmit said, which was broadly seen as welcome news given ongoing concerns over the continent's tight regional supplies for gas and crude oil.

The prospect of a colder-than-expected winter due to an early breakdown of the polar vortex could be "incredibly difficult" for Europe to navigate, Schmit warned. She noted that the region's gas storages have seen "very, very strong withdrawals" over the last two weeks, "which is quite unusual for this time of year."

"If you continue to see the same storage withdrawals over the next couple of months, that means energy prices will spike again — and they won't spike because of geopolitics. This time it will be the weather driving that spike and that volatility," Schmit said.

European gas prices have soared to levels not seen since the 2022 energy crisis in recent weeks, notching 103.9 euros ($113.99) per megawatt hour in late October. European benchmark Dutch TTF futures for December delivery stood at around 87.28 euros on Friday afternoon.

"We have never seen an early breakdown of the polar vortex and a super el Nino in the same winter so we have no historical precedent on how this will impact global gas demand," David Lewis, principal research analyst of gas and liquified natural gas at Wood Mackenzie, told CNBC by email.

El Niño is commonly associated with milder winters, while a SSW event typically leads to colder temperatures and higher snowfall, Lewis said, noting this could materialize in December and January within Europe.

"So far, the start of the European winter heating season has been quite cold but more importantly has seen low wind speeds across Northern Europe," Lewis said.

"The drawdown in stocks in the first half of November has been amongst the highest on record. With inventories starting the winter at their lowest level on record, any further deterioration in temperatures or wind speeds will have an outsized impact on European balances and lead to higher prices across Europe," he added.

Steelers vs Eagles betting guide: Odds, props and picks originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Steelers (6-3) vs Eagles (2-7), Sunday 1:00pm

Point spread/ML: Steelers -4.5/-225 Eagles +4.5/+188

Darnell Washington 20.5 yards, 2.5 receptions

Meanwhile, Bernard had a four-game run of 25 catches before being shutout last week. Warren has 3 catches in 6/9 this season, and 26+ yards in 8/9, he's Rodgers' main checkdown option.

Picks: Smith OVER yards, OVER receptions; Warren OVER receptions, OVER yards

Analysis: Either Steelers TE has scored in six straight. Play them both, just not in a parlay.

Picks: Barkley, Milton, Washington, Freiermuth

#2901. Why one NBA GM reportedly shied away from trading up to draft Yaxel Lendeborg

Why one NBA GM reportedly shied away from trading up to draft Yaxel Lendeborg originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

A Western Conference general manager had Yaxel Lendeborg fifth on his 2026 NBA Draft board, considered trading into the top 10 to take him and then shied away, according to ESPN's Tim MacMahon.

The executive was not identified, and his biggest concern was Lendeborg's age.

"He has to be really good right away or it's a problem," the GM told MacMahon who also reported the unnamed exec's hesitancy to draft Lendeborg was "in part due to worries about how the rookie's 'goofy' personality would fit in his team's locker room."

Lendeborg turned 24 before making his NBA debut, which was the "biggest knock" on him among NBA execs, MacMahon reported. The last lottery pick that old was Chris Duarte, the 13th pick in 2021, who averaged 13.1 points as a rookie and was out of the league after four seasons. Lendeborg has addressed the age talk himself, saying that winning Rookie of the Year would come with a "he's 24" caveat no matter what.

So far, the Warriors rookie has not given anyone a reason to worry. The No. 11 pick ranks second among rookies in scoring (19.7 points) and rebounding (7.3) and fifth in assists (3.9) through 16 games, with MacMahon calling him the likely steal of the draft.

Another West executive told MacMahon it is hard to rule him out as the best player in the class, and an Eastern Conference executive told them he could not have predicted Lendeborg would be this good.

His shooting has helped. MacMahon noted that Lendeborg has improved as a 3-point shooter at every stop, from 33.3 percent in his first season at UAB to 37.2 percent at Michigan and 37.8 percent in the NBA, even with a slump since Steph Curry's injury made him the main focus of opposing scouting reports. Curry has an oblique abdominal tear and was set to be re-evaluated in two weeks.

For the GM who passed, the early verdict is clear: Lendeborg has been really good right away.

#2900. Eagles place Zack Baun, Dontayvion Wicks on IR in roster flurry

Eagles place Zack Baun, Dontayvion Wicks on IR in roster flurry originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Eagles on Saturday afternoon placed LB Zack Baun (back) and WR Dontayvion Wicks (ankle/foot) on Injured Reserve in a flurry of roster moves.

They will miss at least the next four games.

Baun missed all week of practice and was ruled out on Friday. Without him, the Eagles will turn to Jihaad Campbell and Jeremiah Trotter Jr. as starters. Before Campbell missed a couple games with injuries of his own, those two split reps next to Baun. This will give the Eagles an extended look at both of those young 'backers.

Baun, 29, was playing at a very high level again this season. The two-time Pro Bowler had 65 tackles, 2 sacks, 8 TFLs, 5 QB hits and 5 pass breakups in 8 games this season.

Wicks, 25, missed the three games before the bye week with an ankle injury but was able to return to practice this week. He then was ruled out for this game on Friday as the foot injury was added to the report. At least DeVonta Smith (hamstring) will return this weekend for the Birds.

In addition to placing those two players on IR, the Eagles made the following roster moves on Saturday:

• RB Will Shipley (foot) was activated off Injured Reserve, where he had been since Oct. 16. Shipley was able to return to practice this week. He joins Saquon Barkley and Jaydon Blue as the running backs on the roster.

Expect Shipley to slot in between Barkley and Blue in the pecking order. He'll also take a significant role on special teams. Shipley will retake his role as a kick returner.

• Both DT Gabe Hall and LB Anfernee Orji were elevated for the Steelers game.

Hall will be the fifth defensive tackle after the Eagles traded Jordan Davis ahead of the deadline. He's behind Jalen Carter, Moro Ojomo, Byron Young and Ty Robinson. Sunday will be Hall's 2026 debut; he played in two games in 2025.

Orji first joined the Eagles' practice squad in early October. This is his second stint with the Eagles. He hasn't played in a regular season game since 2024 with the Saints.

#2897. Back at 100%, DeVonta Smith defends playing in Panthers game

Back at 100%, DeVonta Smith defends playing in Panthers game originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

DeVonta Smith was back at practice on Wednesday afternoon as the Eagles returned from their Week 10 bye. The 28-year-old was a limited participant but said he's 100% after missing the last three games with a hamstring injury.

Of course, it's probably too little, too late.

The Eagles have lost seven straight games and, at 2-7, find themselves in the basement of the NFC East. Smith took over as the Eagles' WR1 in 2026 but has played in just four of nine games this season.

But Smith on Wednesday defended the decision to play against the Panthers back in Week 6. If you remember, he missed Weeks 4 and 5 with a hamstring injury and then ended up aggravating it against the Panthers and missed the next three games.

Smith claims he was 100% healthy going into that Panthers game, but still aggravated the injury.

"I know someone asked Nick (Sirianni) about shutting me down during the season," Smith said. "I didn't bust my ass this whole summer, I didn't continue to get up at 4:30 in the morning every day, to come out here and not play. My focus has been to getting where I was supposed to be.

"I felt 100% that game. I feel 100% now. Like I say, if something happens, it's out of my control because I know I do everything I can do for my body to make sure I'm in tip-top shape."

Smith was on a pitch count in that Panthers game and ended up playing 42 snaps, which he said was right on target. But he clearly tweaked the hamstring during that game and missed the three division losses against the Cowboys, Commanders and Giants.

In hindsight, the Eagles probably could have waited for Smith to return for those crucial NFC East matchups. Maybe it would have preserved him and the season, but maybe it wouldn't have mattered.

Smith said he has given up on being frustrated about the injury.

"I do everything I can do," Smith said. "I've been doing everything I can do. That's why, myself, I kind of got to the point where I was like, 'Maybe this is what God wanted' for whatever reason it is. Because I did everything I can do and if it happens, then it's out of my control. There's nothing I can do about it."

The one bright spot of Smith and Dontayvion Wicks missing the last three games is that rookie Makai Lemon made the most of his extra opportunities. The first-round pick had the three best games of his young career.

Lemon had 7 for 67 and a touchdown against the Cowboys, 6 for 57 against the Commanders and 6 for 61 against the Giants. Smith has spent his time off the field trying to help his young teammates as much as possible and was impressed by those performances from Lemon.

"The biggest thing is just going out there and knowing what to do," Smith said. "I think he's settled down a lot into knowing what he has to do and things like that. He's going out there, he's thinking less, he's playing a lot faster and I think that's the biggest thing. You can go out there and play fast and play free, you're going to be alright."

The last time we saw Smith in Week 6, the Eagles' starting quarterback was Tanner McKee. So Sunday against the Steelers will be his first game with Joe Milton under center.

Smith said he is using this week to learn as much as he can about the way Milton operates. He also admitted that Milton is still learning the offense.

This is Smith's six NFL season and he has been to the playoffs in the previous five. That streak is very likely going to end in 2026 as the Eagles enter the second half of their season with a 2-7 record.

Smith admitted has been a frustrating season from a team perspective.

"But at the end of the day, it's not over," Smith said. "Especially in this division. I feel like there's still a chance in our division right now but we can only take it one week at a time so focusing on this week. Going out there and getting a win would be the biggest thing right now. Getting back on track."

#2895. Can Cameron Carr and Adou Thiero stay in the Lakers’ rotation?

My defense is that the Lakers were overdue for a loss and the energy and emotions in Tuesday's win over the Hornets eventually came back to bite them. They haven't been convincing in their current homestand and can certainly use a return from Austin Reaves, but things could be worse.

So, let's dive into your questions for this week.

We got a peek at the answer to this when the Lakers were in Milwaukee as head coach JJ Redick and the team were playing an 11-man rotation prior to Austin Reaves' injury.

That feels like stretching things a bit much unless it's at the end of a stretch of three games in four days or a long road trip. It's more likely the team settles on a 10-man rotation once Reaves returns, with either Cameron Carr or Adou Thiero as the swing player. When they went to 11 guys, both youngsters played.

#2891. Rams WR Puka Nacua gets knee surgery, likely out for season

Rams WR Puka Nacua gets knee surgery, likely out for season originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

All-Pro receiver Puka Nacua is likely to miss the rest of the season after undergoing knee surgery, Los Angeles Rams coach Sean McVay said Friday.

Nacua injured ligaments in his knee while carrying the ball on the final offensive snap of the Rams' win over the Washington Commanders on Nov. 8.

McVay had expressed optimism last week that Nacua could return to the field this season, but he said the star wideout decided this week that he should have knee surgery, which was performed Tuesday.

Nacua received a three-game suspension from the NFL for violating its personal conduct policies on Thursday. His suspension will expire during his mandated stint on injured reserve, but the knee surgery will keep him out much longer.

McVay didn't rule out a return by Nacua during the playoffs, but he wouldn't speculate that far ahead.

Nacua has been one of the most productive receivers in NFL history during his first four seasons and has 70 catches for 715 yards and four touchdowns while playing in just seven games this year. He is in the final year of his rookie contract and is eligible to sign a large new deal.

The Rams (6-4), currently outside of the playoff picture, will be without their top two pass-catchers when they host the Green Bay Packers on Wednesday night. Davante Adams also isn't ready to return from his hamstring injury, but McVay is optimistic he can play the following week.

#2889. Walker Kessler flashes ‘f–k yeah’ moment in game of ‘f–k nos’

As Walker Kessler walked into the interview room, he uttered four words.

The Lakers, who have the NBA's best record at 14-3, had eked past the Kings (1-14), who are in the league's cellar.

Walker Kessler (14) of the Los Angeles Lakers shoots a 3-point basket against the Sacramento Kings on Friday, Nov. 20, 2026, in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

For Kessler, the Lakers' 106-104 win on Friday was mostly forgettable.

He got lit into by Lakers coach JJ Redick at halftime after only having six points and one rebound before turning things around to finish with 15 points, seven rebounds and three blocked shots. He missed two free throws with 5.3 seconds left and the Lakers up, 106-104, a blemish that would've been much worse had Zach LaVine not missed his go-ahead 3-point attempt with 0.9 seconds left.

In a few days, no one is going to remember that the Lakers trailed the Kings by as much as 11 points in a game with 10 lead changes and 15 ties. All that anyone is going to remember is the W.

Kessler has already proven himself as an excellent rim defender and rebounder, but on Friday he revealed that there are some more tricks in his bag.

With 11:42 remaining in the fourth quarter and the Lakers trailing the Kings, 84-82, Collin Sexton passed the ball to the 7-foot-1 center in the corner. Kessler then fired a 24-foot jumper which swished through the net.

So, you know, he's shooting a stunning 100% from beyond the arc.

Dylan Cardwell of the Sacramento Kings plays defense on Walker Kessler of the Los Angeles Lakers in Los Angeles, Friday, Nov. 20, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

It wasn't exactly the play Redick called. He said it was "unorganized," adding, "I'm not sure why [Kessler] was even in the corner."

"I didn't bat an eye when he shot it," Redick said.

During the preseason, after Redick drew an ATO that involved Kessler, the center wanted to make sure he did his due diligence by inquiring about all possible scenarios.

Walker Kessler grabs a rebound during the NBA Cup game at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles, Friday, Nov. 20, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

"We break the huddle and he's like, 'Coach, what do you want me to do if the ball finds me and we don't get a layup on this play?' Redick recalled Kessler asking. "And I said, 'Shoot it.' And he goes, 'F–k yeah.'"

So in a game with so many "F–k nos," Kessler finally got his moment.

"I feel good," Kessler said, adding a caveat. "Should've made those free throws."

Zscaler shares surged 18% in extended trading on Friday after Bloomberg reported that CrowdStrike is in talks to buy the cybersecurity vendor. Shares of CrowdStrike shares fell more than 8%.

The talks between the two companies are ongoing, Bloomberg reported, citing sources familiar in the matter. CrowdStrike declined to comment on the report. Zscaler didn't immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

CrowdStrike has been one of the big cybersecurity winners in the artificial intelligence boom, with its stock about doubling in value this year, lifting its market cap to $235 billion. Zscaler, meanwhile, is down 13% this year, pushing its market cap down to about $32 billion.

Along with CrowdStrike, Palo Alto Networks has seen its business boom as businesses race to upgrade their networks in the AI era. Palo Alto closed Friday at a nearly $282 billion market cap.

Should a deal take place, it would be among the largest in the history of the cybersecurity industry. Google acquired Wiz last year for $32 billion. In 2024, Cisco paid about $28 billion for Splunk. Earlier this year, Palo Alto closed its purchase of CyberArk for $25 billion.

CrowdStrike's deals to this point have been on the smaller side.The company announced plans earlier this month to acquire agentic observability platform Fiddler AI and AI trust platform Dreadnode.

#2886. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

Eagles injury update: Offensive reinforcements return after bye week originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

#2883. Is Dillon Brooks the Suns’ most valuable trade asset?

Stabilization. Now that we're 15 games into the season, we're starting to see some of that happen for the Phoenix Suns. They sit at 8-7, albeit as the 9th seed in the Western Conference, with plenty of basketball still to be played. We're beginning to see the vision Brian Gregory had this past offseason manifest itself on the court on a nightly basis.

And we're starting to see fewer people running to the trade machine in an attempt to fix the Phoenix Suns.

Naturally, that's part of being a fan. We recognize deficiencies and attempt to find solutions, however wild or unlikely those solutions might be. Given the tools at our disposal these days (I heart you Fanspo), it's become increasingly easy to explore potential trades. We all do it, and it's part of the fun of following a team. It's also part of the frustration, because many of the proposed solutions are completely illogical. We blindly assume another organization would gladly accept whatever trade proposal we've concocted within the confines of a trade machine.

The same could be said about a recent proposal from Fadeaway World's Siddhant Gupta. In his latest column, he made three suggestions that would send Dillon Brooks to the San Antonio Spurs. One was for Tobias Harris, with the addition of two first-round picks. The second was to send Keldon Johnson and a first-round pick to Phoenix for the Villain. The third? Exchange Brooks for Harrison Barnes, Luke Kornet, and a protected first-round pick.

What was your initial reaction to that one? Disgust? An immediate, "I'm not gonna do it"? My reaction was one I didn't expect to have. It had less to do with the proposed trade itself and more to do with the player involved. It raised a simple question: Is Dillon Brooks the Phoenix Suns' most desirable asset?

Before I dive into this whole thought exercise, I want to make one thing clear right off the bat. I have no desire for the Phoenix Suns to trade Dillon Brooks at this point in time.

He's the heartbeat of this team, the culture setter. And while he can be an inefficient shooter at times and make some poorly timed decisions, his presence is ultimately a net positive that extends well beyond the stat sheet. He helps define who this team is, and when we get an engaged version of Dillon Brooks, he lifts everyone around him. He brings out the energy and identity that make this team competitive and an absolute annoyance for the opposition.

For all those reasons, especially when you factor in his $21 million salary this season, Brooks becomes a highly desirable asset.

Look at Devin Booker. His contract is simply too much for most teams to absorb. At $57.1 million, acquiring him would require quite the haul from an opposing organization. Truth be told, as we've experienced here in Phoenix, having Devin Booker as your Batman doesn't necessarily mean you're going to take over Gotham.

Jalen Green? We've been so focused on different trade scenarios and potential returns that we've perhaps overlooked the reality of his value. Despite his elite athleticism and potential upside at 24 years old, he hasn't demonstrated that he's worth the $36.3 million he's being paid this season.

But Brooks? If you're an organization looking for a culture setter, someone who brings toughness and establishes an identity, Dillon Brooks can provide exactly that. And he does so on a contract that makes him an incredibly desirable asset.

Let's expand on this thought exercise a little further and take a longer view of what a potential Dillon Brooks trade could mean for the Phoenix Suns, without factoring in anything they might receive in return, shall we?

Brooks signed a three-year, $73 million extension this past offseason, keeping him on the books through the 2029-30 season, when he'll be making $26.1 million. As we know, the salary cap will continue to rise over the next three seasons, potentially by as much as 10% annually, with each increase compounding on the previous one. A more realistic projection is somewhere around 6%. Using that figure, the salary cap would be roughly $185 million in the final year of Brooks' contract.

The Suns are currently projected to have $145.3 million committed to salaries that season, which also happens to be the final year of Devin Booker's extension. That leaves Phoenix with approximately $40 million in cap space and five players under contract. Booker, Brooks, Collin Gillespie, Koa Peat, and Oso Ighodaro.

There's one significant factor missing from the graphic above, however. A Miles Bridges extension. Because, like it or not, it's coming. You don't surrender an unprotected 2033 first-round pick to acquire a player you ultimately have no intention of bringing back. Given Bridges' production thus far this season, I believe there's enough to justify an extension, albeit at what I would project to be a manageable number based on how this front office has operated recently.

So let's add a sixth player to the equation and hypothetically assign Bridges a $30 million salary for the 2029-30 season. There you have it. Six players under contract and approximately $10 million in cap space.

Brooks will be 33 entering that season, which by no means suggests he'll have one foot in the grave as a basketball player. However, aging players tend to become less effective and less desirable when you factor in the money they're making relative to their production, along with the increased injury risk that comes with age.

When you look at this from a bigger-picture perspective, knowing that Brooks and Bridges are somewhat duplicative in what they provide on the court, you can begin to talk yourself into the idea that the Suns' most desirable asset could help address some of their immediate deficiencies, most notably size, while also providing long-term relief on the salary cap sheet. It would create financial flexibility, open opportunities that have yet to present themselves, and make those future salary commitments easier to manage.

If you trade Brooks now for someone with only one or two years remaining on their contract, you've potentially cleared $26 million from the books in 2029-30. That gives Phoenix some financial flexibility as it prepares to enter the final season before Bradley Beal's dead money finally disappears. (Dear Lord, it still won't be off the books by then?! Nope. Not until the summer of 2031!)

In short, you're cleaning up the books in preparation for a time when the Suns will finally have money available to make some moves. You're positioning yourself to strike when the right opportunity presents itself.

Who could be available in free agency that summer? Luka Doncic. Anthony Edwards. Mikal Bridges. Pretty much anyone drafted in 2025 who hasn't signed an extension with their respective team.

Look at a team like the Utah Jazz, who the Phoenix Suns played last night. That's a team with an abundance of size. They were without Jusuf Nurkic, Jaren Jackson Jr., Lauri Markkanen, and Kyle Filipowski, yet still had Jaxson Hayes and Mo Bamba providing quality minutes as big men who could rebound and be an annoyance on the glass. I'm not suggesting you ship Dillon Brooks off to Utah, but that's certainly a team that could use a culture setter right now. They looked like a collection of individuals rather than a cohesive unit, although what else can you expect from a team dealing with that many injuries?

But who knows? Who knows where the Phoenix Suns will be on February 8? After all, that's when Brooks becomes trade-eligible after signing his extension this past offseason, only three days before the February 11 trade deadline.

At that time, they'll be closer to getting Mark Williams back, certainly, but how much do we realistically expect him to provide in terms of rim protection when he returns? He'll be coming off a significant injury and will likely be on a minutes restriction as the organization eases him back into the rotation. They recently committed to him with an extension, and the last thing they want is to risk any long-term consequences to his health.

So as we continue playing with our trade machines, and while disappointment may exist with the production of Jalen Green, I believe Dillon Brooks is the Suns' most desirable asset.

Again, I have no desire to trade him at this point. There's plenty of basketball left to be played, and we still have a lot to learn about this team. But if you're a fan looking for ways to improve the Phoenix Suns, putting Dillon Brooks into the trade machine might be the most logical place to start. His contract, production, and presence make it much easier to understand why another organization would be interested in acquiring him. Because every team that plays the Phoenix Suns finds Dillon Brooks annoying as hell. Every one of those teams would love to have a player like him. He's the kind of guy you despise when he's wearing the opposing jersey and absolutely love when he's wearing yours.

I love Dillon Brooks about as much as I love cheesy cauliflower on Thanksgiving. And if you've never had it, you're missing out. Make it with Cheez Whiz. It's delicious. Of course, you should also know that it's one of those dishes that'll have you farting for a week. Which, come to think of it, is pretty much how I felt about that proposed trade sending Dillon Brooks to the San Antonio Spurs. Ferrrrrrrt.

#2881. I never wanted the American Dream and I felt like something was wrong with me—until I moved abroad

I got my first job at 17, working the register at an Eckerd's drugstore in Texas. My manager was only a few years older than me, and while my coworkers seemed motivated by the idea of working their way up to his position, I felt no desire to follow that path.

It all felt suffocating: the added responsibility, the longer hours, the idea that this was the ladder I was supposed to want to climb. I wanted to work, but I couldn't wrap my head around building my whole identity around a job.

I never wanted the American Dream I saw so many people around me striving to achieve. For a long time, I wondered if something was wrong with me. It wasn't until I finally made a long-term move abroad about a decade ago that I realized it was okay to want something different.

After college, I got a job in travel sales. I was good at it. Talking about travel all day suited the wanderlust I'd carried since my first trip abroad in my teens.

I continued working in sales, marketing and the odd temp role for years. But they were always just jobs. I didn't feel like they defined me or feel a calling to an industry or title the way people around me seemed to.

Of course, I wanted financial stability, but I was unwilling to sacrifice my time and well-being to get it. I just didn't connect with the version of ambition that meant grinding toward a corner office. What I wanted the most wasn't status; it was freedom, growth and new experiences.

The wanderlust eventually caught up to me, and at 26 I moved to Spain to teach English. Even though I was working, people saw it as taking a year off from my career and warned me it could damage my resume. I simply didn't care. Living for my resume had never made sense to me.

Looking back, I think being abroad offered an escape from the pressure to chase the traditional path of the American Dream. There, I felt free to enjoy life and figure out who I was without worrying about what box I needed to tick next.

I wanted to stay in Spain, but I couldn't find a visa that would let me live there long-term, so I returned to the U.S. I became a serial job hopper for almost 10 years, quitting a job every year to spend summers visiting friends in the Netherlands, the U.K. and Switzerland or solo traveling in Spain on the money I'd saved up.

On the personal side, I wasn't in a rush to get married or have kids. In my 30s, single and childfree, I felt like I was failing at adulthood, even though deep down, I didn't actually want the things I was supposedly behind on. Settling down just because everyone else was doing it never felt right to me.

And I couldn't shake the dream of living in Europe. In my 20s, people mostly assumed my desire to travel was a phase. By my 30s, the conversations had shifted and I was getting pulled aside. Friends, family and colleagues all had some version of the same message: It was time to get serious and build a life in Texas.

But my gut said to try one more time. So I did.

In 2015, on my fourth attempt, I finally made it to Spain for good. It was scary to shake things up when everyone around me was settling down, but deep down I knew at 35, this was my chance to stop trying to conform and start building a life that felt right for me.

In my new home, the pace was slower. I quickly noticed that people actually took their vacations. Strangers at a dinner party didn't immediately ask me what I did for work. Adults in their 30s, 40s and beyond were out living full social lives. Single, childfree women my age didn't feel like an anomaly.

Living abroad didn't solve everything overnight. I still had to figure out how to support myself. But having the space to define myself professionally without constant judgment changed what felt possible.

In 2017, I launched She Hit Refresh, where I help women over 30 figure out how to move to Europe and build new lives for themselves. It's not something I could have created from anywhere else, with any other experience than my own.

In the process, I've met so many others who left corporate America behind to create something different for themselves. I've seen more women asking the same questions I spent years asking myself. Burnt out, disillusioned with hustle culture, realizing that checking all the boxes of the American Dream they grew up on didn't deliver the feeling they were promised.

We're all figuring it out together; each of us writing a version of a life that didn't fit a standard template. Moving abroad helped me understand that success, for me, was never about earning more or owning more. It was always about more ease, more time, more space, more life.

For years, I thought I was behind. I felt like something was wrong with me for wanting a different kind of life. Looking back, I just hadn't found my place yet.

Cepee Tabibian is the founder of She Hit Refresh, where she's helped over 24,000 women age 30+ plan their move abroad. Grab her free Move Abroad After 30 Guide to start planning your own move. The daughter of Colombian and Iranian immigrants, Cepee grew up in Houston, Texas, before becoming an immigrant herself in Spain. Follow her @shehitrefresh.

#2880. Saquon Barkley healthier after bye, hoping to salvage his season

Saquon Barkley healthier after bye, hoping to salvage his season originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Before the bye week, Saquon Barkley was battling through hamstring and back injuries for a few games.

As the Eagles get ready for the second half of their season, Barkley is definitely feeling healthier. But it doesn't sound like he's 100 percent, nor will he be.

"I wish the bye week was like a magic pill and everything just vanishes," Barkley said. "But that's only in la-la land, I guess. But it definitely did help."

Barkley, 29, has had a disappointing season. Injuries have limited him, but he has just 69 rushing attempts for 296 yards and 3 touchdowns. It's obviously not all his fault, of course. Barkley is playing behind a struggling offensive line in a dysfunctional offense.

But he also doesn't think he is personally playing to his capabilities.

"I think the biggest thing you could do in a bye week is go back and watch yourself on film," Barkley said. "Watch other football, watch other games and see how well other backs are doing. And just be honest with yourself. If I'm being honest with myself, the last couple weeks, for whatever reason, I don't like the way I was playing and what I was putting out on the field.

"The way I go about changing that is the only way I know how to, just put my head down and go to work, just continue to be consistent. But it's got to look different. It's got to look different than what it was in '24 and '25 because every season is going to be different challenges. Felt really good this week to be able to have a full week, go out there and practice and put the work in. And hopefully I see the reward on Sunday."

The last two games for Barkley haven't gone well. Against the Commanders, he had 13 carries for 30 yards and against the Giants, he had 13 carries for 22 yards. His yards-per-carry average dropped from 5.6 to 4.3 on the season after those two losses.

So Barkley and his teammates happily welcomed a bye week on the heels of a seven-game losing streak.

"I think it was helpful for everyone," Barkley said. "From staff to everyone that works in this building to players. Sometimes when a season is not going the way you wanted it to go and you're facing a lot of adversity, that week off is really important. More so for just the mind also. Obviously, the body. It's good for the body. But just the mind and getting your mind somewhere else a little bit. And then regroup and come back ready to work."

As the Eagles look toward the last eight games of the season, they are in the basement of the NFC East with a 2-7 record. They are not technically dead just yet, but it would take a mini miracle for them to pull themselves out of this.

But that's easy to say on the outside. For the guys in the locker room, they have to keep fighting even after they eventually get eliminated. That starts with Sunday against a Steelers team that ranks third in the NFL in rushing defense.

"One game at a time," Barkley said. "I said it already, I'm going to say it again. We put ourselves in this situation. Only way we're going to get out of it is by digging ourselves out. I still believe we have the men and women in this locker room and in this building to get it done. … Technically, we're still in the division and obviously has that crossed my mind? Of course. But can't even take it there.

"Just have to be focused on one game at at time and staying present and taking advantage of the opportunities and this opportunity we have coming up against a really good team who's trying to get back on track and definitely didn't like the way that they played on national television."

With his team lacking the requisite toughness and energy during the first half of Friday's matchup against the Kings, Lakers coach JJ Redick delivered a different kind of halftime message.

And he did so in a way he hadn't done that much during the team's first 16 regular-season games, which they won 13 of.

Los Angeles Lakers coach JJ Redick had a passionate message to deliver to his team during halftime. AP Photo/Caroline Brehman

"I feel I did a decent job of regulating at halftime," Redick said. "They may have a differing opinion of that. Can't repeat every word I used. The main message was, 'We've got to be tougher, we've got to bring a little bit of energy.'"

The level of intensity that Redick brought to the locker room was nothing new for Luka Dončić after having played for Redick over 1 ½ years.

"I mean he yells, but I'm used to it," Dončić said.

But the Slovenian superstar felt that Redick's methods were necessary after the Lakers trailed by eight points to a one-win Kings team that had lost its previous seven games.

"Got us all fired up," Dončić said. "Sometimes you get to yell."

The Lakers responded to Redick's message, winning the second half 52-42 to beat the Kings 106-104 and avoid losing consecutive games for the first time this season.

The team's starting center, Walker Kessler, was among the players who took Redick's message to heart.

Kessler, who recorded a points-rebounds double-double in the previous six games and 10 of the previous 11, had just one rebound and four fouls to go with six points at halftime.

"He lit me up a little bit, rightfully so," Kessler said. "But it was constructive. He was like, 'I know you can do this. Do it.'"

Walker Kessler responded well to JJ Redick's halftime message. Getty Images

Kessler had team highs of nine points and six rebounds after halftime.

His biggest blemish in the final two quarters was missing a pair of late free throws that would've haunted the 7-foot-1 big man and the Lakers had Zach LaVine's buzzer-beating 3-point attempt converted.

But he also had one of the biggest highlights of the second half when he made his first 3-pointer of the season, a pull-up from the left corner to give the Lakers a one-point lead early in the fourth quarter.

"I wasn't playing [physically]," Kessler said of Redick's message to him. "I wasn't going up to dunk the ball. I was missing some more touches around the rim. And then defensively, being undisciplined. Jumping at stuff I don't need to jump at. He definitely lit into me. It was good. I needed it. That's what makes a good coach. Knowing when guys need that and when guys don't."

And it was clear the Lakers not only needed the message Redick delivered but also appreciated how it was delivered after not playing their best basketball over the last week to kick off the four-game homestand that ends Monday against the Jazz.

"At the core of everything that all of our coaches say, and JJ especially, they just speak to the truth of what's happening on the court," Matisse Thybulle said. "And if it needs to be said passionately, that's the message. That's how he needs to get it across, and that's what he does.

"But ultimately, JJ doesn't really mince his words, and he doesn't play games. He really gets to the point, and I think as players, we all really appreciate that. And like he understands, and we understand where he's coming from because he's been in our shoes as well."
