#2823. Cowboys passing attack should shine, pass rush could struggle

The Dallas Cowboys desperately need a win, and Sunday's matchup against the Tennessee Titans offers a chance to get something positive going after all the Christian Parker drama. Tennessee is 1-8 and has one of the league's worst defenses, particularly in the secondary. Dallas should have plenty of opportunities to move the ball through the air, especially with CeeDee Lamb and George Pickens coming off productive performances.

There are still reasons to be cautious. The Cowboys' defense has been a disaster this year, and Tennessee quarterback Cam Ward has improved over the past few weeks. With Dallas also changing its defensive play-calling, this matchup could tell us a lot about the team's direction. Here are two players to buy and one area to sell ahead of Sunday's game.

CeeDee Lamb has been on a tear this year, and this week's matchup against Tennessee should give him another opportunity to put up big numbers, so it's no surprise he's on this list again. Lamb is coming off a seven-catch, 72-yard, one-touchdown performance against San Francisco. That came after his 10-catch, 195-yard outing against Indianapolis.

Through 10 games, Lamb has 77 receptions for 976 yards and eight touchdowns. He's averaging 97.6 receiving yards per game and has become the most consistent weapon in Dak Prescott's passing attack. This matchup is appealing. Tennessee is allowing 266.9 passing yards per game, the most in the NFL. The Titans have struggled to contain opposing receivers, with Cleveland's KC Concepcion, Cincinnati's Tee Higgins and Jacksonville's Parker Washington all putting up big performances against them in recent weeks.

Lamb should have plenty of opportunities to take advantage of that secondary. Whether he's working out of the slot or lining up outside, the Cowboys need to make sure he's involved early and often. With eight touchdowns already on the season, there's little reason to believe his production is slowing down.

I'm not buying the Cowboys' pass rush this week, even after the unit recorded five sacks against San Francisco. That might sound strange, but Tennessee has allowed just 14 sacks this season. The concern is what Cam Ward has been doing with his legs and how the Titans have adjusted their offense to better suit him.

Ward has been much more comfortable over the past few weeks. He's completed 54 of 79 passes for 535 yards, four touchdowns and one interception across his last two games, posting a 98.9 passer rating. He's also been more involved as a runner, and Tennessee has increasingly used tempo and designed quarterback runs to create opportunities for him.

The Titans' offensive approach has changed considerably. They've incorporated more no-huddle looks and opportunities for Ward to use his mobility. Against Jacksonville, Tennessee used no-huddle on 49% of its offensive plays, the highest rate by any team this season.

That's a concern for Dallas. The Cowboys have struggled to contain quarterbacks who can escape the pocket, and their pass rush has too often lost its discipline when trying to generate pressure. If the defensive front gets too aggressive and opens running lanes, Ward has the mobility to make them pay.

There is also a new wrinkle on the Dallas sideline. Mike Pettine will take over defensive play-calling after the Cowboys removed those responsibilities from Christian Parker. Maybe the change helps, but it's difficult to expect an immediate turnaround from a defense that has struggled with execution and consistency all season.

If Lamb is the Cowboys' most consistent weapon, George Pickens might be their most dangerous big-play threat. After missing time with a hamstring injury, Pickens returned against San Francisco and delivered the best performance of his Dallas career.

Pickens hauled in 11 receptions for 145 yards and three touchdowns on 18 targets. He set career highs in catches and receiving touchdowns, giving Prescott another reliable option in a passing game that has been productive throughout the season.

Through nine games, Pickens has 50 receptions for 678 yards and five touchdowns. He's averaging 13.6 yards per catch and has shown the ability to stretch the field and win contested catches. Tennessee's secondary has struggled to contain receivers all season, and its recent performances suggest opposing offenses can find plenty of downfield opportunities. The Titans are allowing the most passing yards per game in the league, and they've surrendered several big performances to opposing receivers over the past month.

With Lamb commanding attention from the secondary, Pickens should have opportunities to exploit one-on-one matchups. The Cowboys have two legitimate receiving threats capable of taking over a game, and this is the type of matchup where both could produce.

#2820. 40-year-old Josh Johnson making historic Cardinals start not seen since Jim Thorpe

It is rarely a positive development for a team that it must start a fourth different quarterback in four weeks.

For the Cardinals, it is somewhat historic.

The quarterback-desperate Cardinals will turn to the ageless but also aged Josh Johnson on Sunday in Kansas City, where the longtime backup will play a game at 40 years and 191 days old.

Arizona Cardinals quarterback Josh Johnson leaves the field after an NFL football game against the Los Angeles Rams in Glendale, Ariz., Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. AP Photo/Ross D. Franklin

According to Mark Dalton, the organization's senior vice president of media relations, Johnson will become the second-oldest non-specialist to play in a game for the Cardinals. To find the oldest, go all the way to Jim Thorpe in 1928, when the legend was 41 years and 185 days old in a Thanksgiving Day game against the Bears (which did not go well, with the Chicago Cardinals losing 34-0).

Johnson is getting a chance because Jacoby Brissett, who started in Week 8's win in Dallas, was traded to the Ravens; Gardner Minshew II, who started in Week 9's loss in Seattle, required season-ending elbow surgery; and rookie Carson Beck, who started in the Week 10 loss to the Rams, suffered back spasms that will not allow him to play Sunday.

So it is Johnson, who was signed off the Bengals' practice squad earlier this month, getting an opportunity to play a game for an eighth different franchise in a career that began as a fifth-round pick in 2008.

Football player Jim Thorpe poses for a photo between 1910 and 1920. HUM Images/Universal Images Grou

"There is no next step to the playbook, this is going back to the starting table of contents, and that's OK," head coach Mike LaFleur said, according to the team's website. "For a three-and-a-half-hour window, it's my job to figure out as you know whatever we can to get the ball moving forward."

Johnson will not become the oldest active player in the NFL. That title belongs to Aaron Rodgers, who turns 43 on Dec. 2 and will start for the Steelers in Philadelphia on Sunday.

#2818. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2817. Jaguars saw something in Liam Coen that other teams didn't

In a season and a half with the Jaguars, Liam Coen has established himself as one of the best coaches in the NFL. Last year, Coen took over a team that had gone 4-13 the year before and improved their record to 13-4 — the best record ever for a first-year coach taking over a team that had won four or fewer games the year before. This year Coen is showing that last year was no fluke, with the Jaguars at 7-2 with an NFL-best point differential of +125.

Coen’s great success should have other teams that hired new head coaches in 2025 asking themselves: What did we miss? Because the Jaguars were so high on Coen that he was their No. 1 target and they were willing to do whatever it took to get him, while no other team even bothered to give Coen an interview.

During the 2025 NFL coaching search, we tracked dozens of reports of head coaching interviews and requests among the seven teams that had vacancies. The Jaguars were the only team that so much as asked Coen to interview.

This week there’s been some media scrutiny on the Jets, who hired Aaron Glenn without considering Coen as a candidate. Glenn is 5-22; Coen is 20-6. But it’s unfair to single out the Jets for failing to interview Coen, because no team other than the Jaguars did, either.

The 41-year-old Coen had a résumé that should have made him a good candidate. NFL teams are always on the lookout for coaches who can nurture a franchise quarterback and run an innovative offense. Coen played quarterback at UMass and coached quarterbacks in college before getting hired by Sean McVay to work on the Rams’ staff, eventually became the Rams’ offensive coordinator under McVay, and had been a successful playcaller as offensive coordinator for the Buccaneers, as well as at Kentucky. With that background, it’s surprising that only the Jaguars wanted him.

And the Jaguars really wanted him, to the point that they were willing to do whatever it took to hire him. Coen initially turned the Jaguars down and agreed to a contract extension to stay with the Buccaneers, but Jaguars owner Shad Khan wouldn’t take no for an answer. When Coen said he was concerned about the direction of the Jaguars’ front office, Khan fired General Manager Trent Baalke and agreed to reshape the front office in a way that Coen would approve of if Coen would take the job. Coen was persuaded to take the Jaguars job, and the Jaguars later hired James Gladstone, who had worked with Coen on the Rams, as GM.

The result has been the Jaguars going from one of the worst teams in the NFL to one of the best. Coen’s success in Jacksonville has justified the faith Khan had in him. And raises questions about the total lack of interest from the rest of the league.

#2815. 49ers WR Mike Evans is "set for his return" on Sunday

After more than a month, 49ers receiver Mike Evans is on track to return to action.

Evans joined the 49ers in free agency, after 12 seasons with the Buccaneers. In six games with the 49ers, Evans has 27 catches for 420 yards and three touchdowns.

#2814. Ask How Many: 3 Observations as Golden Knights Snap Skid with Shootout Win

When a team is struggling, it doesn't matter what kind of win breaks the losing streak; it can be ugly as long as it's a win. That's the sentiment that came out of the Vegas Golden Knights' locker room after they ground out a 5-4 shootout win over the Winnipeg Jets to snap a four-game losing streak.

"It wasn't the perfect game tonight," said forward Tomáš Hertl following the 5-4 shootout win. "It wasn't beautiful, but it's a long season– you play 84 games, and you can't always play a perfect game. But good teams find a way to get two points, and I think that's what we did tonight."

"[It was a] big win," forward Ivan Barbashev agreed. "I feel like we needed that one, especially after the last two games against Dallas… We've been struggling and on a losing streak, but once again, it was a big win."

The Golden Knights fell behind just 29 seconds into the first, then spent the rest of the game trading goals with the Jets. They had two leads during regulation– one in the first period and one in the second– but found themselves trailing after Kyle Connor's third-period goal gave the Jets a 4-3 lead.

Tonight, the Golden Knights did what they did best last season: they came back from a third-period deficit. Ivan Barbashev tied the game late in the third period, and the Golden Knights won in the shootout.

"We always believe that we can come back," said William Karlsson. "It's good for morale, knowing that we can come back and get the win."

When the Golden Knights fell behind less than 30 seconds into the first period, it didn't bode well for a team that was already on a four-game losing streak. At times this year, they've allowed one goal to turn into two and then three, and that easily could've happened tonight.

Enter Brett Howden, who grew up in Oakbank, Manitoba, a town just outside of Winnipeg. At 8:18 in the first period, Jack Eichel won a face-off clean, and Howden snapped a shot past Stuart Skinner to tie the game at one.

That was Howden's eighth goal of the season, tying him with Victor Olofsson for fourth on the team.

Howden's goal gave the Golden Knights new life, and they took their first lead of the night a minute and 26 seconds later. They dominated the first period, outshooting the Jets 16-9 and generating 23 scoring chances while holding Winnipeg to six.

Howden also recorded the secondary assist on Ivan Barbashev's tying goal in the third period.

The Golden Knights didn't play a perfect game tonight. They struggled with turnovers all game, and their top line was on the ice for three of Winnipeg's four goals. In the second period, the Jets thoroughly outplayed them; Winnipeg outshot Vegas 10-5 and generated 13 scoring chances while holding the Golden Knights to just three.

They'll need to clean those things up, but Tomáš Hertl said it best. It's an 84-game season, and it doesn't matter how ugly a win is, as long as it's a win.

"I liked our start," said head coach Ryan Craig following the 5-4 shootout win. "Didn't love our second, but we pushed back in the third. It's a bit of a roller coaster through the games."

"[It was] up and down, I'd say," William Karlsson agreed. "I think we had a pretty good first period. Maybe they were better in the second, and then it went both ways in the third. It was pretty even up and down, but finally we won in a shootout."

When a team is on a four-game losing streak, sometimes it takes a heroic performance to snap that skid. Several players made their presence felt– Mitch Marner, Ivan Barbashev, and the previously mentioned Brett Howden all scored big goals– but no one was more impactful than Tomáš Hertl.

In the second period, Hertl struck on the power play, scoring right after Jets forward Alex Iafallo pushed Victor Olofsson into the net and dislodged it. Hertl's tally was initially waved off, but after conferring, the officials deemed it a good goal.

"At first I didn't see, but [Olofsson] got pushed by [Iafallo], and I still hit it into the net," said Hertl following the 5-4 shootout victory. "I thought it was a good goal, but with challenges, you never know if you'll come up with the call or not. But I'm definitely happy with that one."

Hertl's best play of the night was a fantastic assist on Ivan Barbashev's goal, which tied the game late in the third period. He beat both Kyle Connor and Mark Scheifele in a race to the puck, went into the corner, then threaded a spinning pass to Barbashev, who was all alone in the slot.

"He made a hell of a play," said Barbashev. "Spin-o-rama, between the legs, and I was all alone in the slot. It was a great play, and I had a lot of time and space, so I just shot it."

"We've got some good players on this team," said forward William Karlsson. "That was a great setup [from Hertl] and a nice finish [by Barbashev]."

#2813. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2812. Aidan Hutchinson wasn't fined for sack that injured Drake Maye

As expected, Lions defensive end Aidan Hutchinson wasn't fined for the tackle that may have ended Patriots quarterback Drake Maye's season.

The weekly announcement from the league includes no fines against Hutchinson.

A question emerged as to whether Hutchinson's sack of Maye amounted to a hip-drop tackle. During the broadcast, Fox rules analyst (and former NFL senior V.P. of officiating) Mike Pereira said it wasn't a hip-drop tackle. On Monday, NFL in-house rules analyst Walt Anderson confirmed that it wasn't a hip-drop tackle.

But it's not Anderson who makes the decisions on fines. Jon Runyan makes those decisions. Not surprisingly, Runyan decided that the tackle was not a violation of the rules.

Under the rules, a hip-drop tackle happens when a player "grabs the runner with one or both hands or wraps the runner with both arms; and . . . unweights himself by swiveling and dropping his hips and/or lower body, landing on and/or trapping the runner's leg(s) at orbelow the knee." Anderson said Hutchinson did not "unweight" himself or "swivel."

Hutchinson suffered a torn ACL in his knee while celebrating the sack. Although it's not clear whether Maye will play again in 2026, Hutchinson is done for the year.

#2809. NFL games on TV today: Full schedule, times, channels, live streams to watch Sunday Week 11

NFL games on TV today: Full schedule, times, channels, live streams to watch Sunday Week 11 originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

It's time for the appetizer before the most anticipated week of regular-season football.

With an expanded Thanksgiving week schedule looming in just a few days, Week 11 features only two matchups between teams with winning records.

#2808. Jerry Jones on fan's death after fight at 49ers-Cowboys game: "We had no record of an incident"

This week, a fan died after falling during a fight at AT&T Stadium. The fan was there to watch the 49ers-Cowboys game.

On Friday, Cowboys owner and G.M. Jerry Jones was asked about the incident during an appearance on 105.3 The Fan.

“I just got that information, and so we had no record of of an incident, and we’ll be obviously looking into that,” Jones said. “My understanding is that the individual went to the game and didn’t feel good at the game, and then left, and [had] been hospitalized. But we weren’t aware of any incident that involved a fan at the game.”

They know about it now, and they have the resources to fully investigate.

“We really do have quite a — eons above what it was 10, 15 years ago,” Jones said. “We have quite a surveillance of what’s going on at every aspect of our game. And so it is really rare if we didn’t know of any incident. We’ve got too many people out there. We have 6,000 workers on game day.”

Jones said he’ll pass along any surveillance footage the Cowboys have to investigators.

A full and complete investigation could potentially create liability for the venue. It all depends on the facts. And, frankly, it requires the authorities not to sit and wait for whatever is voluntarily provided but to use the subpoena power to go get anything and everything that will allow the complicated puzzle to be put together as to what happened to Marco Narro, whether it could have or should been prevented, and whether the aftermath could have or should have been handled differently.

#2807. Vikings Can Replace Kyler Murray With $170 Million QB for Next to Nothing

HOUSTON, TEXAS - NOVEMBER 19: Daniel Jones #17 of the Indianapolis Colts calls a play against the Houston Texans during the first quarter of the game at Reliant Stadium on November 19, 2026 in Houston, Texas. (Photo by Tim Warner/Getty Images) Getty

MINNEAPOLIS, MINNESOTA - SEPTEMBER 13: Kyler Murray #1 of the Minnesota Vikings looks on after throwing an interception during the first quarter against the Green Bay Packers of the game at U.S. Bank Stadium on September 13, 2026 in Minneapolis, Minnesota. (Photo by Stephen Maturen/Getty Images) Getty

The Minnesota Vikings may have already seen enough of Kyler Murray this season to know he isn't a viable answer if the team needs to go away from Kirk Cousins for any reason across any period of time.

But the Vikings don't know that for certain about Daniel Jones, which creates the foundation for an argument that Minnesota should sign the Indianapolis Colts' castoff if he clears waivers next week.

Judd Zulgad of SKOR North predicted on Saturday, November 21 that Jones will move through the waiver wire unclaimed, which will land him in free agency and give the QB the freedom to pick his next team. The last time Jones found himself in that situation was the second half of the 2024 campaign, and he picked the Vikings and head coach Kevin O'Connell.

"The Vikings planned to bring Jones back for the 2025 season and have him compete with first-round pick JJ McCarthy for the starting job, but Jones elected to sign with the Colts," Zulgad wrote. "So would the Vikings be interested in Jones at this point? That depends, in part, on whether he clears waivers — something that seems almost certain to happen."

GettyQuarterback Daniel Jones, formerly of the Indianapolis Colts.

Should Jones clear waivers, he will cost his next team just over $500,000 for the remainder of the season. That would prove a considerable bargain given he inked a two-year contract extension worth $88 million total in Indianapolis before the season and has earned nearly $173 million over the course of his eight-year career.

"Any team that claims [Jones on waivers] would be responsible for $2.135 million for the remainder of the season. It also would inherit his $38 million in non-guaranteed salary and bonuses for 2027, along with $10.51 million in salary and bonus money that is guaranteed for injury and would become fully guaranteed next March," Zulgad continued. "But if Jones clears waivers, those financial obligations remain with the Colts. A team signing him as a free agent would be responsible for only $505,556 for the remainder of the season."

At that price, Jones can offer the Vikings an alternative to Murray and Carson Wentz essentially for free. Minnesota has just under $5.3 million in available salary cap space as of Saturday night.

GettyQuarterback Kyler Murray of the Minnesota Vikings.

Murray was also a bargain for the Vikings, as they signed him for a league minimum $1.3 million while the Arizona Cardinals remained on the hook for around $35.5 million of the QB's 2026 salary.

Minnesota has no obligations to Murray beyond this season, and his return is now highly unlikely after the Vikings dealt a third-round pick for Cousins at the NFL trade deadline earlier this month.

Murray will miss his third straight game with a hamstring strain against the San Francisco 49ers on Sunday after sitting out Week 2, and more than three quarters of Week 1, with a concussion. When he has been on the field, Murray has not proven adept at running O'Connell's system.

Wentz had more success doing so last season and in relief of Murray in Week 1. However, he managed just nine points against the Chicago Bears in Week 2 and fell flat against the Buffalo Bills two weeks back with a stat line of 8-for-25 passing with two interceptions.

Meanwhile, the Vikings reached out to the Colts to inquire about trading for Jones ahead of the deadline before they secured Cousins, so there was at least some interest there less than two weeks ago.

#2806. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."

#2805. NFL didn't know Puka Nacua had surgery when agreeing to suspension

The sudden decision of the NFL to suspend receiver Puka Nacua three games raises plenty of relevant questions. On Friday, a technically irrelevant question was answered.

After Rams coach Sean McVay disclosed to reporters that Nacua had surgery to repair a torn MCL suffered during a Week 9 win over the Commanders, Judy Battista of NFL Network reported this: “The NFL did not know Puka Nacua had had knee surgery on Tuesday when it suspended him yesterday.”

Surely, that’s accurate; Judy is a solid reporter. But that extra fact doesn’t matter to the NFL’s ultimate handling of Nacua.

The report creates the impression that, during the negotiations that resulted in the three-game suspension under the Personal Conduct Policy, Nacua and/or his representatives withheld material information. That, at some level, the league had been duped by the lack of knowledge that Nacua had undergone surgery that will sideline him for “a couple months.”

First, the suspension (as the league affirmatively explained on Thursday) is separate from the injury. The NFL listed multiple examples of players who served suspensions while injured.

Second, whether Nacua (who had already missed one of the four minimum games he’s required to miss while on IR), was already going to serve the entirety of the suspension while on injured reserve with or without surgery.

Third, the surgery and the extended absence actually avoids the possibility that Nacua would have returned to action exactly one game after finishing his suspension, resulting in no net games missed due to the suspension.

The mere existence of the report represents an implicit acknowledgement by the league that the negotiated suspension, aimed at allowing Nacua to double-dip when it comes to his stint on injured reserve and his suspension, underscores the broader question of whether players should be allowed to serve a suspension under the Personal Conduct Policy when otherwise unable to play. That’s a separate issue for the NFL to revisit, if it chooses, through a potential revision to the terms of the policy.

As explained on Friday, the league could have refused the deal. It could have insisted on pushing the issue through the full process, with Judge Sue L. Robinson conducting a hearing and the outcome potentially being appealed (by Nacua and/or the league) to the Commissioner. But that would have entailed a non-zero-percent chance that Nacua wouldn’t have been suspended at all.

For Nacua, the goal was to get the suspension served while injured. The fact that he had surgery before the agreement was reached gave him more of a buffer than the NFL knew about, expanding the window during which the suspension could be fully served before the end of the 2026 season without Nacua missing time due to the suspension. Which would still allow Nacua to have this issue behind him before the 2027 offseason, when he signs a new contract with the Rams, has the franchise tag applied, or becomes a free agent.

Of course, Nacua now needs to tread lightly. The league has made it clear that “any further violation of the Personal Conduct Policy, including failing to satisfy the conditions of his diversion program, will subject him to additional and more substantial discipline, including banishment from the League.” To the extent that the league isn’t thrilled by the perception/reality that it allowed Nacua to serve his suspension at a time when he otherwise could not play, the league could be ready to pounce on any opportunity to impose a longer suspension that would apply when Nacua is healthy again.

#2804. 3 alternative starting lineups the Suns should consider

As the Phoenix Suns move into ninth place in the Western Conference, riding a 3-0 record on their current road trip, you might think everything is hunky-dory. That this team is finally gelling in a way that's translating into victories. And I'm not going to argue against that. I think there's merit to it. But that doesn't mean everything is right in Phoenix. It means this team is finding its footing and taking advantage of some inferior competition, as well it should. That's what good teams do.

One thing continues to be clear, however. The starting five that Phoenix trots onto the court every night remains one of the worst in the league.

And I know, I know. It's not about who starts. It's about who finishes. That's something I firmly believe, although when your starters aren't getting the job done, you're forced to rely on your bench to stabilize things. That's hardly ideal. Thankfully for Phoenix, they possess one of the most productive bench units in the NBA, a testament to the team's overall depth.

Still, when your starting unit consistently fails to set the table, you're going to struggle against quality opponents. We've seen that play out throughout the early portion of this season. Starting matters, despite my personal philosophy on the subject. Especially when you factor in the egos of players and the agents who represent them. If someone believes they should be starting, particularly when they're being compensated like a starter, you can bet that any suggestion of moving them to the bench will be met with resistance from an agent who believes his client's value is being diminished.

One thing working in Phoenix's favor has been relative health. I know that might sound strange considering the injury to Mark Williams, but holistically, the starting unit has enjoyed considerable continuity through the first 15 games. In fact, only two teams in the NBA have utilized only two different starting lineups this season. The New York Knicks and the Phoenix Suns.

That continuity hasn't necessarily translated into production, however. When you examine the numbers associated with Phoenix's two starting lineups, they're pretty abysmal. The unit featuring Khaman Maluach has posted a net rating of -11.5 across nine games together. The lineup featuring Oso Ighodaro? An even worse -14.4.

If we're looking for ways to tweak the Suns' approach, exploring changes to the starting lineup might be where the answer lies. Whether that means adding playmaking, improving the rebounding, or injecting a little more attitude into the mix, Phoenix might benefit from experimenting with different combinations if it wants to compete more consistently against the league's better teams.

Now, I understand the importance of establishing continuity. The more time these players spend together, the better they'll understand where their teammates are supposed to be on both ends of the floor. That familiarity builds cohesion, and cohesion can eventually translate into success. So I'm not necessarily advocating for an immediate change, especially with the Suns playing quality basketball and winning seven of their last nine games.

But there comes a point when you've seen enough from a particular lineup to understand its strengths, its limitations, and what it ultimately provides. When that point arrives, it might behoove Jordan Ott to explore some alternatives. With that in mind, here are three starting lineups the Phoenix Suns could experiment with.

I'll start simple here by adding Jordan Goodwin to the starting five.

Goodwin has been stellar this season, and when you look at his on/off metrics, the Suns have been better in nearly every category, including defensive rebounding percentage. His net rating differential sits at +20.7, the ninth-best mark in the league.

Now, you can look at that two ways. The first is that he clearly has a positive impact whenever he steps onto the floor. You feel it. You see it. He's one of those emotional players who has the ability to affect everyone around him, like that positive coworker who walks into the office and immediately makes the day better. Everybody enjoys playing alongside Goodwin because of the effort he provides.

The other way to interpret that number, and probably the way I lean, is that the starting lineup has been so bad that Goodwin benefits from being part of a second unit that consistently outperforms it. When the group you're replacing struggles as much as Phoenix's starters have, it's easier to generate an impressive net rating differential.

The interesting question is where Goodwin fits if you move him into the starting lineup. My choice? I'd replace Dillon Brooks, primarily because Goodwin provides a greater rebounding presence than Brooks currently does. Brooks is averaging 3.6 rebounds per game, while Goodwin is pulling down 4.7. From a rebounding percentage perspective, Brooks sits at 6.1%, compared to Goodwin's 10.9%.

If rebounding is an issue for the starting unit, which it certainly is, adding Goodwin helps address that deficiency while providing a player whose skill set complements what Miles Bridges brings to the floor rather than overlapping with it.

It's also worth noting that a lineup featuring Devin Booker, Jalen Green, Jordan Goodwin, Miles Bridges, and Oso Ighodaro has already appeared for eight minutes this season. The net rating for that combination? +37.5. Yes, it's a small sample size. But within that limited opportunity, the results have been pretty damn good.

If there's one guy I'd really like to see get going, it's Luke Kennard. Through 14 games, he's averaging 17.3 minutes per night, providing 8.6 points, 1.7 rebounds, and 1.6 assists. His shooting? It's been as advertised, connecting on 43.8% of his three-point attempts. The issue is that he's attempting only 4.6 threes per game.

That was one of the concerns when Kennard arrived in Phoenix. Would he be willing and able to become a volume shooter? Because ultimately, that's what the Suns needed him to be. You're replacing the three-point production of Grayson Allen and Royce O'Neale, two players who certainly weren't afraid to let it fly. Allen averaged 8.9 three-point attempts per game last season. O'Neale? 6.7.

Perhaps one way to stoke the flame of what Kennard could become is to insert him into the starting five.

His efficiency has been stellar, although you'd be sacrificing some of what Jalen Green does well and probably doesn't receive enough credit for, most notably his rebounding and defensive length. In return, you're adding another legitimate perimeter threat alongside Dillon Brooks, who has been fantastic from deep to start the season.

You'd have Devin Booker handling much of the facilitation and distribution, although Kennard has demonstrated an ability to contribute in that department as well. He doesn't do it in volume, because quite frankly, nothing he's doing right now comes in volume. But his 12.3% assist percentage isn't all that far removed from Green's 17.3%.

Perhaps the most telling statistic comes from the on/off numbers. In the 325 minutes Jalen Green has been on the court without Luke Kennard, the Suns have posted a net rating of -9.8. When Kennard is on the floor without Green? Across 165 minutes, that number skyrockets to +26.9.

That's a pretty significant difference, and one that makes me curious about what Kennard could provide with an expanded role. I'd be willing to gamble a little and see what that looks like.

All right, I'm going to give you the one you've been waiting for, and you're probably surprised I didn't list it first. Yeah, I toyed with you a bit. Can't bury the lead, you know?

In my opinion, this is one of the most obvious options for the Phoenix Suns. Jalen Green has been inefficient, and much of what he provides offensively overlaps with Devin Booker. If you can somehow convince Green (and his agent, Andrew Morrison) that moving to the bench is a good idea, the Suns could ultimately benefit. And so could Green. If he embraces that sixth man role, becoming the guy who comes in and uses his athleticism to score against opposing second units, it could actually increase his value over the long term. Or it could completely disrupt what has become one of the most productive second units in the league. I don't know.

But when you look toward the future, knowing that Collin Gillespie is likely to be around longer than Green and continue playing alongside Devin Booker, it might behoove the Suns to give him more opportunities to develop that relationship on the court.

Thus far this season, Gillespie and Booker have played 200 minutes together, posting a net rating of -7.7. Clearly, there's room for improvement, and perhaps that improvement begins by giving Gillespie an opportunity with the starting five.

Now, you might be wondering why I'm suggesting starting Khaman Maluach over Oso Ighodaro in this scenario. It's simple. What Oso does well is facilitate from the center position, utilizing high screens and dribble handoffs to create opportunities and open up different aspects of the offense. When you have someone like Gillespie, who can effectively navigate an offense with the ball in his hands, you don't necessarily need that additional wrinkle in your starting five. What you could use, however, is more effective rebounding and shot blocking. That's where Maluach comes into the equation.

We've actually seen this combination on the court this season, although it has existed for only six minutes. But what a glorious six minutes it was. The Suns posted a net rating of +92.3 with that lineup. Clearly, that's not a statistic you can live and die by given the limited sample size, but if you're thinking about the future of this team, getting Gillespie and Maluach opportunities to develop together could be highly beneficial.

Another reason I'm not advocating for Oso in this particular lineup is that we've already seen what that combination looks like, albeit in another limited sample. Across 10 minutes featuring Gillespie, Booker, Brooks, Bridges, and Ighodaro, the Suns posted a net rating of -47.6.

Again, we're dealing with extremely small sample sizes, but I think that further fans the flames of my thesis. Having Oso in a starting lineup alongside Gillespie potentially diminishes some of what makes Ighodaro effective and valuable to this team. His ability to facilitate becomes less essential when another capable ball handler is already providing that element.

Of the three lineups I've explored, this is the one I'd most like to see the Suns experiment with. That doesn't necessarily mean Gillespie and Maluach need to become permanent starters. But at some point this season, I'd like to see them share the floor more frequently so we can gather meaningful data and determine whether those early numbers hold up. And you best believe I'll be keeping an eye on it.

Those are my lineups. Those are my suggestions. Again, they don't necessarily need to be starting lineups. It's fun to suggest three different starting fives that the Suns should trot onto the court, but in reality, we know that's unlikely to happen for the reasons I mentioned above. Agents, egos, and contracts ultimately determine which players get introduced at the beginning of games, with fireworks going off and a gorilla waving a giant flag.

That being said, there's an opportunity for head coach Jordan Ott to weave some of these combinations into games, giving his coaching staff a chance to evaluate how productive they can be. As you can see, there's very little data available on some of these lineups, which leaves plenty of room for speculation. The best way to eliminate that speculation is to actually put these combinations on the court and see what happens. That provides meaningful data, helps us better understand what works and what doesn't, and ultimately gives the coaching staff more information to make decisions that may eventually become necessary.

The Suns have won seven of their last nine games, and that's encouraging. But winning shouldn't prevent Jordan Ott from exploring ways to make this team better. The starting lineup has consistently struggled, and there are intriguing alternatives worth investigating. Maybe none of these combinations provides the answer. Maybe one changes everything. We won't know until they're given meaningful opportunities. Because continuity is valuable, but at some point, continuing to do something that isn't working becomes stubbornness rather than strategy.

#2802. Barlow looks set for season debut with Sixers after long days, ‘miserable' weeks

Barlow looks set for season debut with Sixers after long days, 'miserable' weeks originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Dominick Barlow is on the verge of his season debut with the Sixers.

The team listed Barlow as probable for its Saturday afternoon matchup with the Bucks at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Barlow practiced Friday and has been making clear progress in his recent on-court work recovering from an injury formally classified as a "lower back stress reaction."

He was a reliable rotation fixture last season, playing 71 regular-season games and starting 59. Barlow posted 7.7 points, 4.8 rebounds, 1.2 assists and 0.9 steals per contest in 23.8 minutes per game. The Sixers then made a common-sense call to exercise his $3.4 million option for the 2026-27 season.

The 23-year-old described his back injury as "an overuse thing."

"I guess there was an area or maybe I started my (offseason) workouts too early," he said. "I don't know what it was fully in terms of what caused it. Once it happened, it happened. Nothing I can do about it besides try to correct it and get my body feeling as good as possible."

He called the past few weeks "miserable" as he rehabbed and waited on the green light by the Sixers' training staff.

"The days just felt longer," Barlow said. "Trying to be engaged mentally, which I think I've done a good job with, but the physical aspect wasn't there. I'm somebody that's prided himself on competing, being available. Not being able to do that was hard, but I'm excited to kind of put this past me."

With LeBron James and Jaylen Brown on the team now, Barlow won't start at forward when the Sixers are at full strength.

He's still a multi-dimensional frontcourt piece for Sixers head coach Nick Nurse who may very well get substantial opportunities at backup center.

"Just cutting, playing hard, being ready to make the open shot," Barlow said. "And then I don't think you can ever have too much defensive versatility. I think that's something I can bring, try to help us be more versatile."

When Barlow checks into his first game of the season, he'll appreciate the moment.

"You don't really realize what you have until it's not there (anymore)," he said. "I'm just excited to play. …. Just playing the first stint … that's going to be the main thing I'm looking forward to."

A Sixers official said the following players were all "unrestricted" at the team's light practice:

Dean Wade (left hamstring soreness/tightness)

Bona still needs to practice with contact before a return is on the table, according to the official. Saturday's game will mark his eighth consecutive absence.

Brown and Wade have both been ruled out for the 13-3 Sixers' meeting with the 1-15 Bucks. Maxey was listed as questionable Friday night.

Washington isn't in the running to win the Big Ten, but it could play spoiler to Oregon's playoff hopes. In a rivalry, throw out the records.

Dan Lanning has struggled with the Huskies in his time at Oregon, going 2-3 against the Ducks' northern rival, but the Ducks will be favored at home to get the win and continue their late-season surge.

#2799. As Anthropic shares tank, investors scramble to hedge. It could be very expensive

As Anthropic shares tumble toward their IPO price of $83, investors are looking for ways to hedge. Unfortunately, they may have to wait until Monday, when options on the AI giant are expected to list.

If Anthropic breaks its IPO price, it will represent a 27% decline from Thursday's intraday high of $113. The wild price swings offer clues on how expensive it might be to buy protection on the new tech giant once options begin trading.A 20% intraday move points to an implied volatility somewhere between 100 and 150 for Anthropic options, according to estimates from single-stock market-makers on the Cboe trading floor in Chicago.

Options traders are also looking at how SpaceX traded in the days following its offering for clues on how options might price.

Implied volatility in SpaceX started at 116, but that didn't deter options traders big and small from piling into the contracts tracking the stock, with about 1.7 million contracts traded on the first day for a total options premium of about $2.5 billion. Far out-of-the-money call contracts were some of the most popular trades as the stock soared 40% in its first three sessions.One key difference between now and then? Anthropic is trading against the backdrop of S&P 500 volatility close to 30, compared to 17.3 when SpaceX debuted. "I expect ANT options to be super active," said Tom Sosnoff, co-founder of the trading platforms ThinkOrSwim and TastyTrade. "I expect heavy options volume."One thing Anthropic and SpaceX have in common is heavy retail involvement in both IPOs, with retail brokerages receiving a 20% allocation from SpaceX and reports pointing to between a 15-20% allocation from Anthropic on Thursday. "Round one of the Anthropic IPO easily goes to the individual investor," Sosnoff added. "Retail participants bailed early at the best prices of the day while institutional investors paid up to top off their underwhelming allocations. I believe retail was aggressively reloading in the mid-90s."Further signs of retail demand will become evident when options likely begin trading on Monday. One thing to watch: the spread between out-of-the-money call and put contracts, which was heavily skewed toward calls in the case of SpaceX. "I would be careful comparing Anthropic to SpaceX," said Dennis Davitt, CIO and Co-Founder of Millbank Dartmoor Portsmouth. "From a market-structure perspective, the two are dramatically different. The availability and cost of borrowing will be different, the companies are at very different stages of maturity, and SpaceX has allowed employees to monetize portions of their holdings over time. All of those factors matter when pricing options."

#2798. Will Shane Steichen be back in 2027?

After the 2025 season ended, Colts owner Carlie Irsay-Gordon put both G.M. Chris Ballard and coach Shane Steichen on notice that much was expected in 2026.

Then, one day after the Colts lost to the Titans in Week 6, she fired Ballard and kept Steichen.

Nearly five weeks later, Steichen remains. After next Sunday’s game against the Giants, the Colts will enter their bye. That’s typically the best time for an in-season coaching change, since it gives the interim coach maximum time to get up to speed before the next game.

Creating a vacancy with six weeks left in the regular season carries a separate benefit. The team can get a head start on searching for the replacement. And if ownership already knows the coach will be fired after the season ends, it makes no sense to delay that part of the process.

So here’s the question. Will Irsay-Gordon bring Steichen back in 2027?

It makes no sense to keep a new G.M. from hiring a new coach. Every G.M. arrives with a wish list of coaches who would be pursued, if/when the position is vacant. It makes plenty of sense to give a new G.M. an immediate vacancy to fill.

But what about the Colts this year has made sense? The firing of the G.M. left the team without a seasoned, experienced executive to navigate the strategy of making, or not making, trades. They quickly moved guard Quenton Nelson and defensive tackle DeForest Buckner. They rejected offers for running back Jonathan Taylor and quarterback Daniel Jones. They moved two starting receivers — Keenan Allen and Josh Downs — on the day of the trade deadline.

Steichen has been operating for much of the season with one hand tied behind his back. Now, he’ll have both wrists secured. Whatever the decision will be as to Steichen, it’s unfair to base it on the team’s overall performance since the Nelson and Buckner trades.

Before that point, they took the Chiefs to overtime in Kansas City. They beat the Texans. They manhandled the Commanders in London. They took the Vikings to the limit in Minnesota.

Although the signs point to Steichen eventually being fired, the fact that he’s still there now means he could still be there later. The new G.M. may be expected to work with him.

It’s ultimately the owner’s call. And the owner may have strong feelings about Steichen’s skills and abilities, based on the fact that she’s on the sideline every week.

If she was upstairs, she’d likely be hearing criticism of the head coach during the game. Instead, she’s watching him in his element. And she hasn’t fired him yet.

Throw in the fact that a new coach may not be as welcoming to the owner’s presence in the bench area, and she may be even more inclined to give Steichen (who’s fine with it) another chance. Especially since she systematically began making his job much more difficult the moment the Colts traded Nelson and Buckner.

Moving forward, it’ll be virtually impossible. The fact that Steichen has survived this long could mean that he’ll survive whatever comes next.

#2796. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2794. Prediction markets outlook for stocks in 2026 dims amid AI sell-off

As stocks continue their tech-driven sell-off, prediction markets traders are seeing a bleaker outlook for the remainder of the year.

On platform Kalshi, speculators place just 6% odds that the S&P 500 will cross 8,000 in 2026. On Oct. 13, those odds were as high as 72%.

Contracts ask traders if the S&P 500 will go above various levels in 2026, and they're resolved using data from S&P Dow Jones Indices.

On Friday, the Nasdaq Composite turned negative for the year as investors continued to dump tech stocks, primarily those with exposure to the artificial intelligence build-out. The Dow Jones Industrial Average and the S&P 500 fell into correction territory, too.

The S&P 500 has slashed its year-to-date gain to just over 2%.

The Stock Trader's Almanac in a Thursday note broadly agreed that the hopes for a December rally are fading, with the caveat that a "Santa rally" could still appear at the end of the year.

"The market is under pressure and more downside can't be ruled out," the note said.

The Stock Trader's Almanac sees 2026 following the year-end pattern of 2018, when the Federal Reserve was in a tightening cycle in a midterm year. The Fed is widely expected to hike rates at its December meeting.

In that scenario, the Stock Trader's Almanac thinks more churn is to come. "The low is more likely in mid- to late December than this week."

#2793. Raiders owner Mark Davis hated the Madden movie

The new Madden movie includes a brief, fleeting glance of a young Mark Davis. The depiction wasn’t flattering.

His assessment of the film is far less flattering.

The Raiders owner teed off on the Madden movie, in an interview with John Katsolimetes of the Las Vegas Review-Journal.

“My comment is, it’s full of turducken,” Davis said initially.

“It depends on whether you like turducken or not, and obviously, I don’t like turducken,” Davis said.

“I think it’s really it’s unfair to take people that have passed away and make caricatures out of them, and try to sell it as truth,” Davis said. “They start the movie with a statement saying that all of it’s not true, but some of it is, so it’s confusing what to believe.”

That’s not an uncommon device. “Based on a true story” means that plenty of liberties will be taken in an effort to make the story more compelling.

Mark Davis had one specific concern regarding the shading of the truth. He objected to his father’s reaction to the serious neck injury suffered by Patriots receiver Darryl Stingley, during a preseason game in 1978.

“So John goes over to the hospital afterwards, you know, and really looks after him,” Mark Davis said, “and there’s a scene where my dad is telling John, ‘You don’t need to go over there. You don’t need to take care of other teams’ players. You take care of our players.’

“That’s just so far from the truth. Anybody who has ever known him knows my dad always took care of people behind the scenes. If it was a player from another team had an issue that wasn’t being taken care of, my dad would quietly take care of that even if it was players on other teams. I’m serious. . . . It just it felt cheap to me to build up John’s image at the expense of my father’s.”

Davis praised Christian Bale for his portrayal of Al Davis.

“You can’t blame the script on him,” Mark Davis said. “He tried.”

Mark Davis also didn’t care for the characterization of the iconic John Madden by Nicolas Cage.

“You know, he at times did look like John, but he wasn’t John,” Davis said. “I mean, they made John to be a pussy, and John was the farthest thing from a pussy.”

The end result could be a positive. Mark Davis is now motivated to create something that properly and accurately depicts his father.

“I don’t know what this movie’s purpose was, to be honest,” Mark Davis said. “But you know, it’s making me more and more realize that I’m going to have to put together something on my dad. It could be a movie, it could be a characterization, but at least it’ll be truthful. . . .

“I think I need to do that, or at least let people know that I’m thinking about doing that, so that don’t jump in and think that they can do it first.”

The concerns expressed by Mark Davis regarding the script raise the question of whether he or the league or anyone bothered to review it before allowing the producers to use the Raiders name, colors, and logos in the filming of the football sequences. That’s a critical aspect when it comes to the overall credibility of a film about pro football.

If it looks real, it feels real. Unlike other films that didn’t secure the ability to use actual team names and logos (Any Given Sunday, The Replacements).

And that’s the irony of this. Davis and the league allowed the Raiders name and uniforms to be included in the film, giving it the impression of accuracy and authenticity. Which, in turn, makes the story seem accurate and authentic. Which makes it even harder to separate the truth from the fiction.

Here’s hoping Mark Davis comes up with a way to tell the true story of Al Davis. With no embellishment, fabrication, or Hollywood tricks aimed at making the story seem better than it was.

Because the story of Al Davis would be pretty damn compelling if it were indeed confined to the strict parameters of reality.

#2791. Rams get devastating Puka Nacua update as playoff hopes take hit

The last sliver of hope that Puka Nacua might return for a Rams playoff run has officially disappeared.

The Rams' star wide receiver is expected to miss four to five months recovering from surgery to repair the MCL in his right knee, ruling him out for the remainder of the 2026 NFL season, including the playoffs, according to ESPN's Adam Schefter.

The updated recovery timeline is significantly longer than the initial estimate Rams coach Sean McVay provided Friday, when he suggested Nacua would be sidelined for "a couple of months" but wouldn't completely rule out a postseason return.

Los Angeles Rams wide receiver Puka Nacua reacts after winning an NFL football game against the Washington Commanders in Landover, Md., Sunday, Nov. 8, 2026. AP Photo/Stephanie Scarbrough

According to Schefter, three doctors recommended that Nacua undergo surgery, which was performed Tuesday by renowned orthopedic surgeon and Rams team physician Dr. Neal ElAttrache.

"I thought when the initial injury occurred that this was more likely the direction that this would go," McVay said Friday. "This isn't anything necessarily new on our end."

For Nacua, 25, the stakes extend beyond this season. He's scheduled to become an unrestricted free agent after playing out the final year of his rookie contract.

McVay acknowledged that the decision to undergo surgery wasn't simply about salvaging the Rams' season but protecting Nacua's future.

"There's a lot of thought and conversations that go into whether you're going to do that or not and what's the best plan of action for return to performance for him," McVay said. "Not exclusive to this year but for his career moving forward."

#2790. ‘It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround

'It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers created their two cleanest late-game looks several hours before they needed them.

During Wednesday morning's shootaround in Orlando, Nick Nurse had the Sixers work on offensive material they had not consistently used this season. They tested one of those actions during the first half, returned to it throughout the closing minutes and watched it produce the shots they needed to complete a 17-point comeback.

"We worked on some stuff this morning that we hadn't used before, and they ran all that stuff very, very well and very calmly and with composure," Nurse told reporters.

The Sixers have earned six of their 13 wins after trailing by double digits. They are also 11-0 in games that reached clutch time.

Relying on comebacks is not a sustainable plan. The repeated late-game situations, however, have forced a new roster to respond to different lineups, coverages and responsibilities while the season is still young.

Each close finish gives Nurse more information about which players and actions hold up when possessions tighten. Wednesday gave him a new option.

Anfernee Simons' clinching three in the Sixers' 121-116 win over the Magic was the final result of that preparation.

"That last play, we ran that play in the first half," Simons said. "We worked it a couple of times, and in the second half we ran that play every single time down the stretch, and it worked every time.

"It was a play we ran through. [It's gratifying] especially when it's working consistently and you're getting different shots out of it and the opposing team just don't know what to do.

"We just continue to run the play and get the open shots we need."

NBC Sports Philadelphia video producer and Sixers Talk podcast co-host Kevin Rice broke down how the Sixers used its different options to keep Orlando guessing.

The action begins with two players stationed near the elbows. A player on the opposite elbow then sets a screen for the player up top, who receives the ball while moving around it.

The Sixers ran the set three consecutive times during the first half. None of the possessions reached the primary option. Each still produced a quality opportunity.

On the first, Joel Embiid cleared LeBron James out of the area and directed Simons around him for a dribble handoff. Simons received an open three-point look.

Orlando took away the handoff the next time down but left Embiid one-on-one against Wendell Carter Jr. Embiid created enough room to retrieve his own miss and convert an and-one opportunity.

The third attempt pulled Orlando's defense into the paint. Two passes moved the ball through the collapsing coverage and created an open three for VJ Edgecombe.

"We had a little thing where he comes flying off behind Joel, and he doesn't get to do that that much, right?" Nurse said of Simons. "So tonight he gets the opportunity to do it, and it was very good for us on a number of plays."

The Sixers changed the action late without abandoning its initial Horns alignment. Simons moved through the lane to help form a Spain pick-and-roll involving James and Embiid.

On the first late possession, Simons remained near the play after James gained a matchup against Carter. Orlando struggled to sort out its assignments, leaving James open for a 19-foot jumper with 54.8 seconds remaining.

After Jalen Suggs made one of two free throws, the Sixers returned to the same setup. This time, Simons relocated to the left corner after James drew Carter on a switch.

James drove into the paint. Suggs lost track of Simons while responding to the penetration, and James sent the ball toward the corner.

Simons buried the open three to give the Sixers a 119-113 lead with 15 seconds left.

"Bron just penetrated the paint," Simons said. "It kind of got some confusion, and when I was on the dunker and kind of spaced out to the corner, I ended up finding myself wide open for the three, and Bron found me."

The Sixers entered the night without Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown, their two leading scorers. Simons made his first start with Philadelphia and recorded 18 points in the city where he grew up.

His final basket also illustrated why Nurse believes the Sixers have more to uncover with him.

"When he's out there, they gotta be really careful," Nurse said. "So I think there's some drives he creates, and just about every time he lets it go, whether he's on balance or not, you think it's going in, and that is a big deal. Like, that is a big deal.

"And again, I think we're still a ways away from really unlocking him to being even more part of some of the things we can do with him to create more shots."

Philadelphia is now 11-0 in clutch games. The finish in Orlando provided a more specific explanation than experience or shot-making alone.

The Sixers installed a set in the morning, explored its counters and trusted it when the game tightened. James supplied the patience to read Orlando's coverage. Embiid occupied the middle of the action. Simons moved without the ball, and Edgecombe remained ready to shoot.

James said his heart rate drops when close games increase the pressure on everyone else.

"I mean, experience," James said. "A guy that's seen every situation this league has to offer. It's nothing I haven't seen. And, you know, in the moments where people's heart rate goes up or anxiety goes up, mine is pretty low. It's actually lower, you know."

The Sixers still have more actions and reads to learn as Nurse becomes familiar with his new personnel, but their latest win showed their innate ability to adapt in big moments.

"I think we're still a long ways away from knowing all the reads and all that stuff because it's just a little early, right?" Nurse said.

#2789. Wales boss Wilkinson rules out Fishlock return

Rhian Wilkinson says she has not considered asking Jess Fishlock to come out of retirement even though the Wales legend will be on camp for the Women's World Cup play-off final against Norway.

Fishlock ended her playing career earlier this month after her Seattle Reign side lost to Gotham FC in the National Women's Soccer League (NWSL) play-offs.

The 39-year-old played on for one final season at Reign having retired from international football in October 2025.

Fishlock will be part of Wales' coaching staff for for next month's two-legged tie against the Norwegians, who come to the Cardiff City Stadium on Tuesday, 1 December, before the return leg in Oslo four days later.

Despite some speculation over whether Fishlock could play some part on the pitch, Wilkinson said: "I think you get retirement right when you know there's still something in you to give."

The Wales boss added: "It takes years to recognise that you got it right, because you're sort of [thinking] 'oh should I've kept playing', but when you get it wrong injuries and someone else makes the decision that you're finished, that's a tough way to to end your career.

"I think Jess has qualified [for Euro 2025], made history with Wales, done such a great job as well as building the game over in the US and equally so much in Wales.

"[But] she's retired. Jess is here to further her technical and tactical awareness, but also for her experiences and that kind of confidence that she brings to the environment."

While there will be no Fishlock, Wales will welcome back a number of experienced players from injury for the Norway games.

Wales are hoping to cause an upset as they bid to reach the Women's World Cup for the first time, though beating former champions Norway will likely not be enough to guarantee a place in Brazil next summer.

The complex nature of the qualification process means that Wales may have to negotiate inter-confederation play-offs next February to secure a World Cup place, even if they triumph over Norway.
